An exciting scene occurred on the towpath at Hamp- ton Court on Wednesday. A man wanted by the police was walking by the riverside, when two policemen attempted to arrest him. He dodged them,and jumping into the river, swam away down stream. A looke r-on got a boat and rowed after the man. The swimmer was soon caught up, but he made some resistance, and was only brought to the bank with difficulty.

[ALL HISTORIC SCENES IN THE

COMMONS,

HENRY W. LUCY (" Toby M.P." of Punch).

The first time I chanced to be present when the ordinarily still waters of the House of Com- mons were broken up by sudden storm, happened about thirty-one years ago. Mr. Gladstone was in the course of his first premiership, and the historic Parliament elected in 1868 was slowly dying. On what should have been a quiet Tues- day sitting Sir Charles Dilke rose to move for an enquiry into the Civil List, the extravagance of which he had a short time earlier denounced at a public meeting. Sir Charles, standing at the corner seat of the Front Bench below the gang- way, since occupied by him under varied cir- cumstances, got along well enough. He at least managed to make himself heard. But what he said got the back up of a loyal majority, and when Mr. Auberon Herbert rose to second the motion be was greeted with persistent cries of" Vide,' vide ' vide. " . . .

He had, however, come to stay, and meant to deliver a carefully prepared speech. As he stood and shouted the beginning of sentences, the cro*ded House, now in for a lark, persistently bellowed. This was the occasion when, as wa, written at the time, the late Cavendish Ben- tinck "went out behind the Speaker's Chair and crowed thrice." Certainly cock-crowing was added to other more familiar Parliamentary ob- servations. After the row had continued some ten minutes, a member jealous of the reputation of the House, spied strangers. The 6allerie, were cleared, and when they were re-opened it was announced that Sir Charles Mike's motion had been negatived by two hundred and seventy six votes to two.

MR. PLIMSOLL MAKES EIS MARK.

Three years later, having succeeded to the Premiership, Mr. Disraeli found himself con- fronted with a scene even more dramatic than that which distressed Mr. Gladstone in 1872. August was at hand, and the usual statement of Bills thrown overboard was made. Amongst them was the 'Merchant Shipping Bill, in charge Mr. Plimsoll. In those days it was open to an aggrieved member to dislodge ordered busi- ness and arbitrarily occupy the time of the House by moving the adjournment. Mr. Plim- soll availed himself of this privilege, beseeching the Premier, in a voice choked with emotion, not to consign some thousands of men to death. The Merchant Shipping Bill was designed to check the practices of a class of men whom Mr. Plim- soll described as " ship-knackers," who bought at a low price unworthy ships, insured them, sent thena tosea' • and when they foundered, as they usually did, pocketed the spoil. Mr. Plim- soll's excited demeanour, his scarcely veiled ac- cusation of a particular Member, wrought the House to a high pitch of excitement. The Speaker repeatedly calling him to order, Mr. Plimsoll darted forward, waving aloft a piece of paper, and standing in the middle of the floor he gave notice of intention to ask the President of the Board. of Trade whether certain ships, the names of which he read from the sheet of paper he held in his hand, " are owned by and whether that gentleman, —-- is a Member of this House." Amid the uproar Mr. Plimsoll's voice was heard shrieking, "I am determined to unmask the villains who sent these men to their graves." The Premier, showing disposition to rise and say a few words, Air. Plimsoll, still standing in the middle of the floor, shook his fist at the right honourable .gentleman, and said something that was drowned in the uproar. The member for Derby was suspended. But before the Session was terminated, the Merchant Ship- pin.. Bill was added to the Statute Book, and to-day the Load Lioe skims over every sea on which a British vessel floats. THE HcSAO-TtiltKISH WAR. In the Session o 1878, at the full tide of what was at the time knOwn as Jingoism, the House of Commons was one February night stirred to its profoundest depths. The Government—had asked for a Vote of Credit for six millions, avowedly a menace to Russia, at the time at odds wlth Tur- key. Mr. Forster, on behalf of the Fron Oppo- sition Bench moved an amendment equivalent to refusing the Supply. On the seventh of Feb- ruary the House met amid rumours that the Russians bad occupied Constantinople. Ques- tioned by Lord Hartington, nominally leading the Opposition, with Mr. Gladstone fuming' nn his flank, Sir Stafford Northcote, Leader of tho House, read a telegram just received from Sir. Layard, British Minister at Constantinople, stating that the Russians were pushing on to Constantinople, and had compelled the Turks to evacuate important defences. The House recog- nised in this announcement the sound of the war trumpet. Mr. Foster recognising that the as- pect of affairs in the East was one of profound gravity, asked leave to withdraw his amendment. Whilst conversation was going forward a letter was passed along the Treasury Bench till it reached the hands of the Chancellor of the Ex- chequer. Profound silence fell on the excited House as Sir Stafford rose. In faltering tones he said he had received from the Ambassador or Russia official intimation that there was not a word of truth in the statement of which Mr. Layard had made himself the communicant. The state of tension relaxed, Members fell into a state of merriment, not wholly free from sus- picion of hysteria. The first Session of Parliament elected in 1880, was marked by a series of unexpected en- trances and tumultuous exits by Mr. Bradlaugh. Banquo wasn't in it with the Member for 'North- ampton. In the course of a month or so, mem- bers grew quite accustomed to see him enter, walk swiftly down the floor, take his seat, to be presently haled forth. On the 22nd of June a motion to permit him to take the oath was neg- atived by 275 votes against 230. On the next day Mr. Bradlaugh walked up to the Table and demanded to have the oath administered to him. After some discussion it was agreed that he should be heard at the Bar. There he stood for twenty minutes addressing a crowded silent House in terms I that frequently rose to the height of passionate eloquence. Having made an end of speaking he bowed to the Chair and withdrew, whilst the House considered his ap- plication.

Mr. Gladstone rtsinc• to nic,ve the first reolu- tion found himself (aced by Mr. Dillon, who re- fused to resume his seat when called UPOII the speaker. He was forthwith named, and oc- dered to withdraw. This he refused to do until a posse of messengers under command of the Sergeant-at-Arms approached. Thereupon he surrendered. On his withdrawal Mr. Parnell moved that Mr. Gladstone be not heard. A scene Pf indescribable tumult followed. Mr. Parnell was named, ordered to withdraw and, respectfully refusing, the reserves were again called out. Four venerable messengers, whose united, ages probably was not a day less than two hundred and fifty years. advancing to the charge, the Irish Leader surrendered at discre- tion. The farce was carried out till the whole thirty-seven were expelled, a process which oc- cupied an appreciable period of the sitting. These were the good old days, of which we see no more, under the prosaic operation of the latest batch of Standing Orders.

HENRY W. LUCY.

THE NEW YEAR.

Warrington's Welcome.

No novel scences characterized Warrington's welcome on Saturday night, to the New Year. Certainly there• was the usual ringing of merry peals on church bells ; and the sounding of buzzers ";- the customary hearty greetings and seasonable boric wishes ; the letting in "of the New Year by young bloods, but these are the regular accompaniments of the advent of tne New Year,and all were made manifest in the early hours of Sunday morning. At several churches watch. night services were held at the hour of midnight on Saturday though the number of such services was fewer than in past years, no doubt owing to the fact that New Year's Day fell upon a Sunday. There was a large congregation at the midnight set.- vice which was held at St. Paul's church, and which was conducted by the Revs. W. Bracecamp, T. W. Barber, and R. H. Bowen. The address was given by the Rev. W. Bracecamp from the words "Choose you this day whom ye will serve," the preacher strongly urging his hearers to choose between God and the world. A few minutes before 12 o'clock the congregation joined in silent prayer and the new year having been ushered in with the ringing of the church bells the Vicar wished all a happy and prosperous new year, expressing the hope that trade would revive and that those who now felt the pangs of hunger, would find ample, employment and that to them the new year ig al,l in reality be happy one. The service ended lAtu. the hearty singing of the " Te Deum."

_ HILL CLIFFE BAPTIS'r CHURCH. On New Year's Day services of a special character were held in Hill Cliffe Baptist Church. In the morn-. in the Pastor addressed the children on the motto "I'll help to make you happy this year" and preached on "The art of being happy," taking as his text, Luke xii. 15. In the evening the Rev. J. S. Hughes preached on "The key to the problem of right living" (Matt. vi. 33). Suitable hymns were rendered throughout the day, and the choir sang "God holds the key of all unknown at night."

===== 0002601_19050107_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
WYCLIFFE CHURCH.

Mr. A. Corney conducted a well-attended watchnight service, basing his address on the words: "Rejoice in the Lord, and let:your forbearance and gentleness be known to all men, the Lord is at hand." He remarked that it was the custom for people to look back too much upon things which had come to pass, instead of looking forward to the days that were to come. Let them pray that Christ would draw nearer and nearer to them in the days to come, speak words of peace to the nations, and teach men and women how to live more sociably together. Let them co forward and meet Christ without any fear or trembling and hope that He would save their souls.

During the last few minutes of the old year the con. gregation engaged in silent prayer, after which Mr. Corney extended to all his wishes for "A Happy and Prosperous New Year." 'BAPTIST CHURCH, GOLBORNE-STREET.

Golborne-street Baptist Church was filled on the occasion of the Watchnight service conducted by the Rev. H. Dunnico. Selecting for his text the words" If God be for us, who can be against us ?" the preacher said the Apostle reviewed all things in the light of God's love as revealed in Christ, and though he did not shu t his eyes to the existence of pain, and sorrow and suffer. ing, these things did not cloud but even clarified the idea of a good God and he was certain that all things were working together for good to those who loved God. Years had passed away since Paul pronounced his judgment, but the old familiar sights met their eyes. Tribulation and famine, sorrow and death, were in their midst. There wail a shirking of responsibility, and an amazing amount of apathy ana indifference among professing Christians. At times even devout men lost faith and thought that God had done to sleep, but the cross was the pledge that it was not so. And so as they entered upon another year knowing not what i the future days had n store, he commended to them the Apostle's supreme faith in God. They moved in an age which was not yet righteous, but God who made it, and governed it, was the Christ in whom they trusted did not lie sleeping in an obscure crave, but was risen and enthroned calmly waiting for His kingdom to come. When the pace seemed slow, and the march lingered let them not be discouraged. The same power that created the world would ere long redeem it. The last few moments of the old year, and the first of the new were spent in prayer, and then Mr. Dunnico wished his con- gregation " a very, happy, prosperous and joyous new year."

A PAINFUL PORTRAIT.

[Told by a Photographer.]

•• A Photographer is born, not made," said a Camera Artist. He is born to endure as many troubles as the rest of mankind. Mr. Arthur F. Brookes, of 25, The Arcade. Wolverhamption, has travelled through many countries with his camera,but in London his profession career was threatened. " I was ill,"he said, " and none of the cloctors knew what was the matter. But Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people found my trouble and cured it. I was terribly run down.' My nerves seemed to have left me, and at sight of food my stomach would turn. When I forced down a-little food to keep up my strength, the pains in my chest were excruciating. " Two eminent doctors said I was suffering horn nervous prostration, and ordered complete rest. I was was under them six months,but soon after was as ill as ever. I had such pain in my back as to make me cry out and clutch at the first thing I came to. "I attended a hospital for two months, and my case puzzled even the clever doctors here. Reduced to a mere skeleton (for eating was impossible) I lingered ire a dreadful state until four months ago I met an old friend who asked me why I didn't try Dr. Williams" pink pills—they always set him up. I purchased a bor, and it made me a great deal better. By the time I had finished a second box I was rapidly putting on flesh. My appetite returned, and I enjoy my meals now, with- out any ill after-effects. My improved health, after only three boxes, is a revelation. I have not felt so well for over ten years as II do at the present time. There has never been the least return of the pain." Nerves broken down by worry and work set up the most difficult form of chronic indigestion. Broken nerves are nerves starved for the want of blood. Dr. Williams's pink pills for pale people—genuine only with full name, seven words—make blood. They banish anamia and the pains that afflict the weaker sex. But they are equally valuable for men,having cured indiges- tion, !palpitation, bile, nervous depression, sciatica, rheumatism, paralysis, St. Vitus' dance, and nervous disorders. Two shillings and ninepence for one box,or thirteen and nine for six sent to Dr. Williams' Medicine Co., Holborn-viaduct, London, will bring the pills by return post. But they can be had at medicine shops,if readers insist on the genuine. It is a Painful Portrait that the Photographer whose case is described above gives of himself. His experi- ence should be a guide to others.

ERADLAUGH IN POSSESSION OF THE HOUSE. The majority being inexorable, the Speaker directed Mr. Bradlaugh to be summoned in or- der to hear its decision. Called by the Sergeant- at-Arms he walked up to the Table, and stood there, whilst the Speaker remaining seated com- municated what had passed, and ordered him to withdraw. Mr. Bradlaugh was the only per- son in the House who showed no sign of excite- ment. " I beg respectfully to insist on my right as duly elected Member for Northampton," he quietly said. " I beg you to administer the oath, and I respectfully refuse to withdraw." The Sneaker rising to his feet as he addressed the Hoh.§e asked to be clothed with authority to deal with this new turn of affairs. Mr. Glad- stone being deserted by a section of his fol- lowers, sufficient to make the Leader of the op- position master of the situation, Sir Stafford Northcote moved a resolution investing the Speaker with such power as was requisite to the enforcement of his authority. The House di- viding there was presented the unique spectacle of a person officially declared to be a stranger standing solitary in a House emptied of Mem- bers passing through the Division lobby. The Forthcoming Royal Weddings.—lt is seldom that Royal engagements extend over so long a period as that of the German Crown Prince, whose wedding ie fixed for March 22nd, the anniversary of his gloat grandfather's birthday. As a rule Rayal engagements are announced only a few weeks before the wedding, but in this case there will be ample time for the elabor- ate preparations which are being made at the German Court for the ceremony. A New Departure in Street Etiquette.—The story comes from Sweden that Society women there have decided to relieve men of the necessity of raising their hats to ladies in the streets during the winter time. It seems that in many of the towns neuralgia, toothache and influenza are much more prevalent among men than women, and it was suggested that this is due to the obligation imposed on men to doff their hats in the streets, it seems rather a far fetched idea, but the women have made it known that they will be satisfied with a military salute, as long as the cold weather lasts. Possibly they will have to be satisfied with it for good and all,-as,laiving got in the thin end of the wedge, the men may come to think that the conventionality may be just as well dispensed with altogether. At the Sales.—The "Bridge Coat" Is quite a feature In the majority of the Sales, and will be found an exceedingly useful garment both by players, and non- i players of the game. The "Bridge Coat, ' closely 1 resembles the fashionable little coffee coat of a year j ago, and for a quiet little dinner—at home, or at a restaurant—and the theatre afterwards, it is both suit- able and modish-wear. Cream, or "champagne" tambour net is the fabric most in request for the "Bridge Coat, ' the trimming consisting of lace and ribbon, the slips being of ivory silk or satin. These dainty little coats, with their pretty sleeves, falling away from the elbow, so as not to interfere with cards or counters, may only , be worn with white skirts, or skirts that match the net , in hue, otherwise the effect will be patchy, and there- fore impossible from a modistic point of view. The , clever homeworker will be careful to secure useful remnants of this tambour net, which for dressy blouses, is most successful. Fashionable Stationery.—Coloured notepaper is out 1 of fashion, an item to be grateful for, as there is always 1 • suggestion of vulgarity about highly-tinted stationery. Society women are using white or cream notepaper of a rather large size, the envelopes being more than square, and ornamented with a monogram. Single half sheets, for notes, are much patronised nowadays, indeed they may almost be said to have taken the place of the , one time popular correspondence cards. These half 1 sheets bear the address and monogram, as also do all letter cards and post cards, that- find a place on the fashionable woman's writing table. The Craze for Real Flowers.—Florists are rejoiciAg at the revival of the "nosegay," without which no modish r* woman dee her toilette complete. In the morning It is true, she is content with a neat little cluster of violets, snowdrops, or a rose or two, which nestles amid her furs, but in the afternoon she requires a much more elaborate arrangement of blooms to match her toilette, one spray finding a place among the folds of her. blouse, or vest, the othr on that airy fairy creThon, the "dressy" muff. For the evening, the smlrtest women are wearing none save natural flowers, which is a very decided mistake, as a ball frock,for example, elaborately trimmed with real bloom and foliage, is not usually a thing of beauty by the time it has gone through several dances in a crowded ballroom. The careful admixture of the real, and artificial, is by far the wiser procedure, and when the latter fire really choice and scented speci- mens, few could or would detect the deception. The County Balls.—This month is the season for the connty and other great balls, one of the most,importaut being tha Hunt Ball at Tring which took place last Wednesday. There was a large muster from the various seats of the Rothschllds, who own some five or six seats in the neighbourhood. Large parties were taken by Lord and Lady Rothschild from Trine Park. by Mr. and Mrs. Leopold de Rothschild, from Ascott Hall, and by Mr. Alfred de Rothschild from Halton Hall. Lord

A YORKSHIRE CASE. OF PERSONAL INTERBST TO YORKSHIREMEN.

Mr. S. WILSRAW. Rib ble Bank, Settle writes "You will be glad to know that VENO'S SEAWEED TONIC has worked wonders in my case. For the last two years I suffered very much with pain after eating. My stomach was so weak that I could not eat solid food. I lost a great deal of strength and flesh, and was not able to work. I had taken all kinds of medicine; doctors gave up trying to cure me. It was a happy day for me when I started taking VENO'S SEAWEED TONIC ; after the first week the pains were relieved, also the wind. I began to relish my food, and gradually got stronger; now I feel myself cured and able to work." VENDS SEAWEED TONIC Cures the worse and most obstinate cases of stomach, liver and kidney diseases. Annnal Congregational Tea,

The annual tea and meeting of the above church were held on Monday last %hen about 230 sat down to the tables, this number being considerably augmented for the after meeting. The Rev. H. Dunnico presided over the gathering and was Apported by the officers of the church. In the course of his address he said that in re- viewing the work of the past year they had much cause for thankfulness and though there were certain elements in existence still about which he complained a year ago they were of less importance and could now be more easily ignored. The last year had marked an epoch in the history of the church and never had its (influence been so great as it was now The membership was larger, the congregations were larger, and the receipts were higher than last year, and that was a record year. Every institution in connec- tion with the church was in a healthy condition and all round the work was flourishing. He thanked the n Ricers for the way in which they had worked, but thought that the members on the whole had not been as loyal to the work of the church as they ought to have been. Many of his most loyal supporters and best friends were 1 outside the pale of the church. Mr. Dunnico inadvert- ently remarked that three-fourths of the total income ' had been received from persons other than church members. The time had come when their constitution ; must be revised and provision made for all followers of I Jesus Christ who so desired to take a part in the work of the church. He urged the me nbers to realise I the obligations,of their profession and appealed to the young men and women to join their ranks and uphold him in his work. The Secretary, Mr. H. Buckley, then gave a report of the year's work in which he said that 1 the progress had been so great that it was difficult to give any adequate idea of what had been done. It had Ibeen the best year they had ever had and the 1 uture I was rich with hope.—Mr. Balmford in presenting his I report said that this was his last and his best. The total income from all sources reached about £430, the free- will weekly offerings realising £297. Several addresses were given by the various officers and at the close several presentations were made to Mr. Norman Balm- ford on his removal from Warrington to Dewsbuty. The rest of the evening was spent in social entertainment, Miss Turner's mandoline band giving selections. Mr. Brat also gave several Hens on the gramophone.

PREMIER BICYCLES FOR THE POST OFFICE The treatment accorded to the bicycles in daily use. by the telegraph and messengers employed by the General Post Office is notably F. evere,and for a machine to come successfully through the ordeal is a strong testimony to its strength and good quality of materal and workmanship. It is therefore of interest to note that The Premier Cycle Co., Ltd., which in July last secured a large con- tract for the supply of bicycles to the G.P.O. has just obtained another order for a considerably increased quantity of" Premier" bicycles from the same depart- ment a His Majesty's Service.

The conferring of the lionor,ry degree of Doctor of Laws on Princess Louise, Duchess of Argyll, by the University of Glasgow draws attention to the number of women who have in iate years been thus honoured, a very siguira.:ant mark of progress in the position and status of wonum in the world of thought and learninz. Most people will have felt that in this particular instance there was a singulat fitness in the distinction conferred. on account of the Princess's marked Artistic abilities. Among those conferred in receat years was the degree of Doctor of Music on the Princess of Wales by the University of London. Miss Flora Stevenson, whose services to the cause of the higher education of women, and of education generally, are well known, was granted the honorary degree of Doctor of Laws by Edinburgh University; and Miss Jane Barlow, whose Irish stories and sketches are so deservedly popular, is an honorary Litt.D. of the University of Dublin, as is also Mrs. Sophie Bryant, whose work in the cause of education .snnot be too highly praised while Miss MulvalaY, ever a champion of the movement for the higher education of women in Ireland, received the honorary degree of LL. AN EVENING ROBE.

The sketch depicts an evening robe in old-rose chiffon velours. The bodice is draped across the figure,•thedrapiry being carried down to a deep point in front. The plain, sweeping skirt is laid in deep folds on each side, and in a treble box-pleat at the back. The bodice is almost entirely covered by a point de gauze berthe, which falls in a point

back and front and over each shoulder. This is headed by: a double garniture of Banksia roses, intertwining to form ovals, with centres of the old- rose velvet. Except for the lace that falls over the arms the gown is sleeveless, and, as will be i noticed, it s cut very decolleti. The toilet should be completed by a wreath of Banksia roses worn round the hair, and surmounted by a pink osprey.

GOSSIP.

With the New Year Lady Colin Campbell takes editorial charge of the Ladits' Fitld.

Mrs. Finlay contradicts the announcement of her engagement to Lord Henry Fitzmaurice Chichester.

Clever bridge players seem to multiply. Mrs. Arthur Sassoon has lately made a name for herself, and so has Mrs. Torquhil Matheson, who is one of the daughters of the Countess of Aylesford by her first marriage.

Mrs. William James and Mrs. Arthur James are going to tour all over the country with an amateur theatrical company. The piece they have chosen to play is "The Duke of Killicrankie."

THE PENALTY OF COURTESY.

Society ladies in the town of Haparanda, in Sweden, have just arrived at an interesting decision on a point of street etiquette. They have decided to relieve men of the necessity of doffing their hats to ladies in the streets as long as the cold weather lasts. It has gone forth that during the winter all that will be expected from men will be a military salute. The ladies took this step. says a cor- respondent, as the result of studying medical statistics, which established the fact that in winter there are three times more men than women suffer- ing from cold, neuralgia, toothache, and influenza.

DRESS ACCESSORIES.

Dainty dress accessories always prove an accept- able present to a girl. They smarten up a winter dress or blouse, and are easily made at home by clever fingers at a trifling cost. The belt shewn in the sketch is of white kid, embroidered in a flower design in coloured ribbons and silks, and has a gilt buckle and slicies. The turn-down collar, which is something like a boy's Eton collar in shape, has a broad hem-stitched border, and the corners ornamented by a circular piece cut out and filled in with a spider's-web pattern, worked with a coarse linen thread. There are turned-back

in the centre is a little ,Jffee sack, composed entirely of wide lace. The lower frill is set on to a square yoke of net, a touch of colour being given by the velvet ribbon of emerald green which outlines the neck, and which forms the tiny bows. A yoke and berthe of ribbon and lace is made in the handkerchief style. The small yoke 'is composed of satin ribbon faggot-stitched together, the lace berth being bordered by ribbon matching that used for the yoke, but of a greater width. An old fashion revived is the leg-of- mutton sleeve, which is to be especially used for the newest tailor-made costume. A NEW WAY TO GROW BEAUTIFUL.

One cannot help thinking, writes "Ambrosia" in the World, that the present craze for gymnastics for women is being carried to ridiculous excess. It surely cannot be essential to health, happiness, beauty, and everything that a woman covets that she should hang daily on parallel bars, climb ladders, and turn aerial somersaults by the aid of rings. Without doubt, a little of this sort of thing Is excellent, and probably the female form divine is the bettes for having all its muscles properly exercised; but it is heresy to the beautiful women who have preceded us—to our graceful ancestresses, with their delicate complexions and healthy physique—to say that it is only by gymnastic exercises that women can attain to true loveliness. Are we, then, to believe that Albion's daughters have never been lovely until the present time If zomplexions cannot be managed and graceful carriage produced without physical drill that would tax the strength of a seasoned "Tommy," and if eyes are not to be brightened save by acrobatic feats, then indeed must Englishwomen own that they are beaten by the women of other nations.

TO-DAN'S RECIPE.

MOCK CRAB TOAST. —round 2oz. of good soft cheese with a dessertspoonful each of essence of anchovy, made mustard, and vinegar, a dust of coralline pepper, a drop or two of tabasco, the yolks of two eggs, and about 2oz. of butter. Mix this all well till quite smooth (it should be as stiff as good batter), then spread the mixture on squares of hot and well-buttered toast pretty thickly, and bake for eight or ten minutes. Dust with coralline pepper, and serve very hot. This toast possesses the merit of being equally good hot or cold.

"THE VAMPIRE,"

G. B. BURGIN

(efuthor of" lazier's Little Maid," " Fortune's Footballs," 4e.)

PART I.

Mrs. Quigge was a cross, tetchy, perverse oil Londoner, whose acquaintances—she had no friends—alluded to her, when they took Mc trouble of mentioning her at all, as " The Vam- pire." She also possessed a somewhat startling originality of character—an originality which took the shape of making herself as unpleasant as possible on every conceivable occasion. Indeed, her ingenuity was so great in this respect that it has passed into a proverb, and her impatient ex- clamation of "Go on !"—an exclamation signi- fying incredulity arid derision—had gradually become shortened to " Game !"—a limitation in length which made the phrase even more objec- tionable than before. _ .

Most of the dwellers in " Moberley's Mud- dies" (they had been originally known as " Mo- berley's Models ") hated Mrs. Quigge with a deadly hatred born of impotent fury, for ste scorned the conventionalities and made herself equally unpleasant all round. Indeed, when she outpoured the vials Of her wrath, it was, as one old woman with more originality than Biblical knowledge remarked, "Enough to make Joshua himself stand still," although it had the reverse effect on the inhabitants of " The Muddles." They generally sought safety in flight down the nearest Court until the storm abated. Which was wise ; for Mrs. Quigge's adjectival fluency was appalling in its breathless incoherence. She had, too, an objectionable habit of following up her victims as they went about their daily avoca- tions and cif compelling them to listen to her grievances. If they failed to evince a proper amount of neighbourly sympathy, she promptly regarded them as enemies and treated them with more bitterness than the original offenders. And she had an exasperating way of threatening timid young matrons with " casting a spell on them "if they did not lend her " tuppence for a go of gin." As for her garments, they were kidescribable. At some prehistoric period, they had been, pre- sumably, of different colours. Now, the benefi- cent influence of Time had blended them int< one subdued, olive green—a tint which was um obtrusive, and did not attract attention when Mrs. Quigge wished to take her walks abroad. She wore one shoe and a slipper, and her bonnet, which was minus a string, generally hung down her face the wrong end in front, the remaining rusty fastening creating in Mrs. Quigge's mind the delusion that a fly was tickling her nose. When possessed of the necessary funds, she generally smoked an old, short-stemmed clay pipe. Even in the coldest weather, her cos- tume never varied. As she found it com- fortable enough and suited to her require- ments no one dared point out to her how much better looking she would be if properly dressed and tidied up a little. In her youth, it is conceivable that Mrs. Quigge had not been bad- looking ; but a too constant adherence to spirit- uous compounds and an utter indifference to the effect of the London climate, had given her an aspect of vicious ugliness. Which _was a pity. for Mrs. Quigge was a woman, and had been known to smile under the mollifying influence ol a judicious compliment from the big policeman at the corner. Nobody knew how Mrs. Quigge originated ' In the beginning of things there had been Mrs Quigge and " The Muddles." Now it war thought to be " The Muddles " and Mrs Quigge. " The Muddles " had been repaired again and again. Not so Mrs. Quigge, althougl it wail generally admitted that she " bad a tilt loose." Besides, she was partly bald. She else required whitewashing, mentally and physically, although there was no one ii. the buildings boki enough to undertake the task. Of late, things had not gone well with Mrs Quigge. Slie hated innovations. Walter Dear was an irmovation so was Miss Shenstoue therefore, Mrs. Quigge hated them both. .Whin business had these " toffs " to come down to her " Muddles " and "lord it over people as 'ad right to be there? They aren't after no good,' said Mrs. Quigge, oracularly. " Don't tell me. Game !" And so people did not tell her. Willa was the use of telling a woman like Mrs. Quigg. anything—% woman who merely said " Game r and refused to listen to reason unless her delibera- tions were assisted by' means of unlimited gin [mil water, the more gin and the less water the better? Miss Shenstone was librarian of a charitable institution in the East End of London. She had taken high honours at Girton, and was rich. But site was an enthusiast, and, in her enthusiasm— an enthusiasm which bad been fired by the peru- sal of Sir Walter Besaut's East End story of " All Sorts and Conditions of Men "—she dragged old Miss Carmichael, her aunt, to live with her at " The Muddles." Four rooms were transformed into an elegant flat, with beautiful furniture and accessories, so that the old lady might be thoroughly comfortable. Then Miss Shenstone set about reforming everything and everybody, with an enthusiasm which brought a smile to the lips of Walter Dean. As Miss Carmichael never went out, it made 710 difference to her where she lived. She thought that time would cure her niece's enthusiasm : but Miss Shenstone had been at "The Muddles" rather more than a year without experiencing any diminution in her enthusiasm. Every hour of her sojourn there was a grievance to Mrs. Quigge. There would come a day of reckoning, that elderly matron hinted darkly —a day when the "Mother of the Muddles " could not be ig- nored. Inde;sd, when Mrs. Quigge had gone to bibulous extremes she threatened to "do for that poetry chap Dean." He, too, was another griev- ance.

With all the rashness of youth, Dean had quarrelled with his father in order to " earn a living by his pen." Most of us know what that means. Some men, after having led the life of a toad under a harrow, oome out all right in the long run. Some die under the harrow or disap- pear. But Dean did not mean to disappear. If his father chose to extend the olive-branch, well and good. He would perch on it in the most dove-like manner. But until then, he preferred to remain where he was. So he rented a couple of rooms in " The Muddles," and employed Mrs. Quigge to "do for him," and Mrs. Quigge " did" for him in more senses than one, her great idea of making him comfortable being to pilfer his tea and sugar and any other trifles which might be lying about, trifles which in her eyes assumed the aspect of lawful perquisites. One day, however, Mrs. Quigge, in order to celebrate one of her too numerous birthdays, partook freely of an absorbing beverage known by the dwellers in "The Muddles" as " gin and cloves." It was unfortunate that Isabel Shen- stone should cross her path at this juncture. " Game !" said Mrs. Quigge, who looked more picturesque than usual as she executed a wild, unsteady war dance round the beautiful Isabel. " Game ! Game back to yer lords and lordlings, yer dooks and dooklings : and don't come a- muddlize and a-meddlin' down 'ere where yer ain't wanted. Game ! I say. Game back 'ome, and don't come improvin' of us down 'ere. We don't want improvin', we don't, Game !" Isabel recoiled for a moment, with an instinc- tive desire to avoid the unpleasant old creature. A slight flush of Worn rose to her pale but beau- tiful cheeks. She gathered up her dainty dra- peries, and attempted to pass Mrs Quigge with- out attempting to bandy words with so degraded a specimen of womanhood. "Don't you demean yerself like that before your betters," said Mrs. Quigge, and stood ag- gressively across the narrow passage as if mean- ing to hold it against all comer... Suddenly, she was seized from behind. " Mrs. Quigge, if you dare say another word I'll fling you out to the cats in the court-yard !" ("Just as if I was a painted Isabel." said Mrs. Quigge afterwards.) And ‘VAlter DeaTi hustled her into a corner. " You can pass quite safely, Miss Shenstone,” he said. They 1.1 often met at the library. Isabel, recovering b, 1. I 1 In I 11 ief. t this timely aid. Passed with a • whil. Dean kept guard over the shapeless Mrs. Quigge. "And you call yourself a woman :" he said, with scathing scorn. " You call yourself a woman— you—you—animated old gin bottle !" t• Game !" said Mrs. Quigge, with impotent fury. " Don't call me names you—you long- penwiper ! Give me tuppence for as—a drink. You don't know a lady when you sees one." She sank down in a corner of the passage and went to sleep, whilst Walter looked at her curi- ously. " It makes one's blood boil," ha said, addressing Mrs. Quigge's unconscious form, " to think that that beautiful girl, whose days ought to be passed among scenes so different from this, ' should waste her life vainly attempting to elevate masses. Fancy elevating that !" and he looked at the unconscious Mrs. Quigge. Mrs. Quigge opened one eye. " Game !" she said, and went to sleep again. To an unprejudiced observer, Mrs. Quigge ap-

pearect sutiiiiiently elevated already-. gm tb, harried away, someone touched Dean on tie arm. It was Misa Shenstone's maid with a pencilled note.

"Dear Mr. Dean," it said, "Thank you so much for your good offices just now. Will you dine with us this evening F Ify aunt wishes to thank you also. Yours sincerely, label Shen- stone."

" With much pleasure," he said, turning to the maid. "At what hour ?"

" Seven, Sir." Walter looked at his Iv4Atch. " I shall 511. t have time to cleanse myself from the defiiiu; touch of that old hag," he said, turning frni the prostrate Mrs. Quigge. "To think that the same God made Miss Shenstone and Mr.. Quigge ! It seems incredible ! Perfectly in- credible !"

" Game !" murmured Mrs. Quigge, moment- arily opening her other eye. " 'Ag Who's a 'ag ? Ag yerself !" Moved by a sudden feel- ing of compassion for her age and infirmities, Dean roused the prostrate Mrs. Quigge and carefully escorted her to her lodgings, at the same time getting a " lady friend " of Mi. Quigge's to make her a cup of tea and La,ten her return to the paths of rectitude. Even then. Mrs. Quigge was implacable, and after par- taking of her tea entreated her friend to bear witness that the attention sms Quite voluntarj on " The Penwiper's " part.

PART 11.

As far as externals went, there was nothing to indicate that the dinner was in the East in- stead of the West End of London. The rooms were rather small, but decidedly cosy. Miss Carmichael had known 'Walter Dean's father "He always was an obstinate man when he wanted anything," she said, " I sup- pose you take after him, or you wouldn't be here'

Walter looked at Miss Shenstone, and thought that he could be very obstinate indeed with re- gard to her should circumstances ever enable him to speak out. For some months past he had been living in a dream, when he had time to dream, which was at infrequent intervals. Ant; he was really making his way. He found the library a very pleasant room at which to work. Miss Shenstone and her assistant were most con- siderate. He had the run of the place, and coula stay there all day in a comfortable nook, by the huge fire. And latterly, Isabel Shenstone had become very much interested in the pale intel- lectual face of the dark-haired young fellow who stuck PO per-everingly to his work. Indeed, it was at her instigation that be had sent stories to a certain raper, and that they had been accepted. He did not know that Isabel had intimated to the Editor at a dinner-party that the stories should be attentively considered. The Editor, who waa on the look out for " rising talent," with many misgivings had consented to read the stories. Past experience did not incline him favourably towards the work of unknown men. But Waite, Dean's stories were very good indeed ; the Editor wanted as many :more as the author could pro- duce. Owing to this sudden demand, Walter's circumstances bad so far improved that be fe:t justified in leaving " The Muddles," only, some- how, he did not care to do so; and the reason why be did not care to do so was Isabel Shen- stone. Still, he was poor, and she was rich. He could not speak yet. " I have been telling my niece," said the sil- very-haired old Miss Carmichael, " that I an, tired of " The Muddles." There have been two ghastly murders iii the neighbourhood. It not a fit place for either of us to live in. Isabel lias given the experiment a fair trial. We have iced down here a year. She has a following of twenty or thirty people who prey on Int.. I admit that the children love her. How- ever, it is time she resumed her proper position iu Society. That is the fault of Girton. It um- tits girls for Society. They always want to be doing something useful instead of sitting down and waiting for what Providence chooses to send them. In my young days it would have been considered indecorous not to sit down and wait. Don't you think it very unfeminine, Isabel ?" Isabel seemed paler and more tired than usual. " I have come to the same conclusion, aunt," she said. " I meant well, but I am a failure. All my strength seems to have faded away : it must be the bad air. We will go down to the country somewhere, and take a pretty cottage. I can't bear to think of these horribl. murders. They haunt me. It makes one despair of human nature to hear of such sickening butchery. What do you think, Mr. Dean ?" As Walter looked at her,, something in his glance brought an unusual colour into Isabel's cheek. " You are right to leave here," lie said, sorrowfully. "It isn't fit for gently- nurtured ladies. In your exquisite goodness and desire to benefit your fellows, you have borne all this without a murmur but the time has come when you ought not to bear it any longer. You will kill yourself." Miss Carmichael had left the room. Isabel turned away from his earnest glance. "There is only Miss Carmichael who would la- ment me," she said almost inaudibly, as she sank into a chair.

He came round to her side. " Pardon me, that is not so. Everyone who knows you here would find it a very sad day when you left ; but you must leave. I—l implore you." "Why ?" " For your sake—and—and mine." She rose to her feet, confronting him. " You have said too much or too little. Do you wish me to go? If you want me to go, I will go, but —but—"

"Yes," he said steadily. "I wish you to go. Some day I will tell you why should—" He hesitated.

" Should what F" "Should I be justified in doing so." She turned aside.

------ " Before you go," he said, "I should like to tell you what an angel of mercy and light you have been to me. I came to this desolate hole— this vile den of misery. want, and crime—utterly friendless and alone. Yoa helped me in a hun- dred ways, you encouraged me to persevere, you IFOSO me fresh heart and hope and life. To urge you to go away from me is like cutting off my Land. Yet it is best that you should go. Were you to remain I could not—No ! No! I must not speak. Good-night and good-bye. Some- day I will come to you with a—a prayer for you to grant or refuse. Until then—farewell !" Site gave him her hand as the tears struggled to her eyes. The man VMS hopelessly proud. He loved her, and yet he would not speak 'because of his poverty. "You are very proud," she said. "Good- bye !" When Miss Carmichael entered the room, she found that Dean had disappeared. "I was looking for an old photograph of his father," she said. "It is very rude of him to go away. I like him so much. Don't you, Isabel P" " Yes," said Isabel, simply, Miss Carmichael took the girl in her motherly arms. "Is that it P" she asked. " Why shouldn't it be so, Isabel ?" " He is proud ; and I could not unsex myself by making him speak. Auntie, let us go away. I have made my experitnent and failed. Let us go home." "If the lad is like his faller," said the old lady, decisively, "he will speak some day. And now let us make our arrangements for leaving. This place has been too much for you." " Yes." Isabel gazr.d thouirlitfully into the fire. "It has bee,, too ninell for me. Murderers and thieves are all very well in fiction, but they are not pleasant to meet in the slums behin,4l " The Muddles." Mr. Dean must go away also. The district is not safe."

PABT 111.

The next morning, the inhabitants of " The Muddles " were electrified to learn that Walter Dean had been arrested for the murder of a poor Polish refugee who had sought shelter in " The Muddles " and whose body had been found lying by the gateway with the skull smashed in. There was no mistake about it. Mrs. Quigge had also disappeared from " The , Muddles ' in the custody of a stalwart and un- emotional policeman. " Game !" she said to the jeering crowd which accompanied her upon her way, turning upon them like a female Jonathan Wilde. " Game 'once ! I'll be back in a' hour. It's me as informed agin"im !" Things looked very black against Walter Doan, for a cloak dabbled with blood had been found in his room. This cloak was known to have been worn tho night before by the murdered man. Mrs. Quigge testified that she had gone into Walter Dean's sitting-room at eight in the morning to put it to rights and had found the . cloak under the sofa. It " give her a turn," she said, for she had already heard of the mur- der, and she at once fetched the police. Directly this revolting accusation was made known to Isabel she telegraphed to a certain famous solicitor, who {1 US with her in an hour. Two or three mysterious-looking people glided about in the vicinity of the murder. They re- turned with the result of their enquiries to the solicitor, who smiled, and informed Miss Shen- stone that she need not be uneasy with regard to Walter Dean

At the Mice Court proceewngs, hem mac mule afternoon, Mrs. Quigge was asked if she knew the nature of an oath. On her proceeding to give examples with singular fluency and volu- bility,.. she was hastily checked by the presiding magistr4te. At 'length she was sworn and re- peated her evidence, which seemed perfeetly clear. On going to her work that morning, she had seen part of the cloak projecting from Walter Dean's sofa, and there was blood upon it —human blood.

" Accused, have you anything to say ?" asked the presiding magistrate, after learning other de- tails.

" Only to declare my innocence, and to state that the witness., who has a grudge against me, probably put the cloak there herself," said Walter. "She must have taken it off the dead man's body before the murder was discovered. "Gammon !" said Mrs. Quigge, from the back of the Court. " Game !"

The magistrate looked incredulous. "That is precisely what I am about to prove," said Miss Shenstone's solicitor, turning to the Bench. " I produce a witness named Jacob Ray. who will corroborate this statement—l call Jacob Ray." Jacob Ray darted into the box as if he had been there before and knew all about it.' He was a sharp little urchin of twelve. At seven o'clock that morning, just before the discovery of the body, he was passing through the narrow passage opposite "The Muddles," alid saw Mrs. Quigge come out carrying the loak in her hand. Even then she was not sober, and he heard her muttering that she would " plant " the cloak on someone whose mune he did not catch, and get even with him " for shocin' of her about." _

The urchin's story was corroborated by the caretaker of " The Muddles," who said that the old woman had been very tipsy the night before and had wandered into the covered archway op- posite " The Muddles " to sleep off her de- bauch. Mr. Dean had not left " The Muddles " since the evening before his arrest. " I apply for a warrant against this woman for perjury," said the solicitor quietly. " The police are ori the track of the real murderer. His unhappy victim was supposed to have many precious stones concealed about him and these constituted the temptation to kill and plunder him." As a consequence of the solicitor's high-handed and unfeeling action, Mrs. Quigge did not re- turn to her friends "in a' hour." She was afterwards tried for perjury, convicted, and, worst of all, before beginning to serve her sen- tence, made to take a bath. The shock ultimate- ly proved too much for her sensitive constitution. "There is hope for you yet if you repent of your wickedness," said the gaol cliaplin. " Game ! Not after that bath !" retcrted Mrs. Quigge, with undiminished spirit; and cli,l hating Mr. and Mrs. Walter Dean with unrelenting vigour up to the very last moment.

Cuticura.

If there were not another external skin disease known, eczema would be a sufficient infliction on mankind. It per- vades all classes, and descends impar- tially through generations. While some are constantly enveloped in it, others have it confined to small patches in the ears, on the scalp, on the breast, on the palms of the hands, on the limbs, etc., but everywhere its distinctive feature is a small watery blister, which discharges an acrid fluid, causing heat, inflamma- tion, and intense itching, scaling and crusting. The Cuticura treatment is at once agreeable, speedy, economical and com- prehensive. Bathe the affected parts freely with hot water and Cuticura Soap, to cleanse the surface of crusts and scales, and soften the thickened cuticle. Dry, without hard rubbing, and apply Cuticura Ointment to allay Itching, irritation and inflammation, and soothe and heal, and, lastly, take Cuticura Resolvent, or Pills, to cool and cleanse the blood. This treatment af- fords instant relief, permits rest and sleep in the severest forms of eczema and other itching, burning and scaly humours, and points to a speedy. per- manent and economical cure of tor- turing, disfiguring humours, eczemas, rashes and inflammations, from infancy to age, when all other remedies and the best physicians fail.

I There is no immedi THE PRICE OF PEACE. ate prospect that

the fall of Port Arthur will hasten the conclusion of peace. Mili- tary opinion in St. Petersburg inclines to the belief that the prestige of the Russian arms must be vindicated. This is a very natural view. We remember what was commonly thought in this country after the early disasters in South Africa. But the case of Russia presents some material points of difference. The CZAR is not only fighting the Japanese; he is virtually at war with his own people. The domestic enemy is even more formid- able than the foreign enemy. Of course, the enmity in Russia is not directed against the CZAR himself, but against the system which maintains Li the nineteenth century a form of administration worthy only of the most barbarous period of history. It is no use telling us that the Russian people are ignorant and unfit for self-government. Their ignorance is the most damning part of the indictment against the autocracy and bureaucracy. Their natural leaders—in other words, the whole educated class outside the official world—are exercising the most strenuous pressure in favour of constitutional re- form. Nobody can pretend that the Zemstvos are ignorant. They may be said to correspond to the middle classes who extorted the Reform Bill from the reluctant Sovereign and the House of Lords in 1832. A Russian journal truly says that the war is disastrous because the co-operation of the People and the Government is lacking. But that is the fault of the Government. The CZAR and his advisers have acted on the principle that the people have nothing to do with the laws but to obey them. As TOISTOY pointed out in his letter to the CZAR three years ago— the letter which was published in the " Times " on Tuesday—this principle cannot be upheld any longer without violent disruption. The letter was prophetic. The disruption has come ia a form most terrible to any ruler. At a time when Russia has suffered the worst of a long series of disaster at the hands of an Asiatic foe,her people refuse to their Sovereign that loyal and patriotic support without which her power is in danger of paralysis.

The plain truth is that the Russian people want peace now because a victorious war would simply fix more firmly upon their necks the bureaucratic yoke they yearn to shake off. That is the root of the whole matter, and it nullifies the arguments drawn from the assumption that the Government and the people have a common interest in the struggle with Japan. They can have no common interest in anything so long as the CZAR rejects the petitions of the most enlightened of his sub- jects,and imagines that his duty is to hand on to his son a system which could be justified only by the personal will of Peter the Great, and by the political conditions which prevailed in his time. It is pathetic, therefore, to read the assurance from St. Petersburg that Russia cannot entertain the idea of peace until she has won a victory. "No victory, I no peace," is the formula. We do not suppose

that Marshal OYAMA will permit General 1 KIIROPATKIN to beat him just once in order that the military party in Russia may have this excuse for proposing terms. The situation of KIIROPATICIN grows worse. There is no reason to suppose that he is getting all the reinforcements he needs, and he is said to be complaining with increasing bitterness of the delays in trans- port of all kind. Even if he should be able to mass the 400,000 men airily assigned to him by the Russian Embassy in Paris, where the difficulties of feeding such a host at the end of the Trans-Siberian Railway do not seem to be appreciated,he will have to face a confident enemy of at least equal strength. " Noals victorious troops," says the Russian Embassy, "must be very worn after their long campaign." This de- lusion is pathetic. Troops who are very worn do I not capture fortresses like Port Arthur. But nothing is sadder in the whole business than this Iclutching at the mirage of an ultimate victory.

BY MESSRS. HICKMAN & OTHERS.

''.-M

As events turned out the most contentiols topic at the Council meeting on Tuesday w& the account for the printing of the Non-Trading Com- missioners' report, which had been rejected by the General Purposes Committee, and came up on the minutes of the committee. There was an acrimonious discussion, opened by speakers who appeared to derive a peculiar pleasure from the fact that they were opposing something in which the Non-Traders were concerned, and carried on by Councillor Pem- berton, who, whatever may be urged against him, never fails to stand to his guns. The whole affair is paltry, and by no means to the credit of the Council. We make bold to say that had the , Council Commissioners compiled so long a report and, in their honest judgment decided that the best form in which to present it was in print, the Corporation you'd have gladly accepted it and paid the bill without a murmur.

It has been decided by an overwhelming majority in the Town Council that an extra half- penny fare shall be paid for the new route between Stafford-road and Victoria-square,Stockton:Heath. Anything else could hardly have been expected, except by the f' Heathens,' who are given to expecting a lot. The comparison which is made between the Latchford and Stockton Heath routes is not a fair one, and it is no argument to say that because the length of the latter is :less than that of the former the fare shall be no more. There is a vast difference between the cast of the two Iroutes, and the Corporation is bound to consider , the question of profit and loss as well as the convenience of the public. Moreover, the new line at Stockton Heath extends beyond the Borough boundary for almost the whole of its length, and this is an additional reason why the fare should be increased. Really, there ,is no justificatiou for the Council to extend a tramway, at the cost of £32,000 a mile and do it for nothing. Stockton Heath people have not earned such a boon. Their " cusaedness," as Comcillor Maginn calla it, has frequently bean a source of vexation to the Corporation of Warring- ton. All things considered we think the charge of an extra halfpenny a fair and reasonable one. By the time the " Heathens " have earned grace and virtue, and the Corporation a considerable hoard of pence, the question may be re-opened. •

The Water Committee has succeeded in obtain. ing sanction to take the preliminary steps for borrowing £20,510 in order to complete the water- works undertaking, Councillor Pemberton was practically the only opponent of the pro- posal, and he was unfortunately prevented from making a strong case by the fact that he has been e'denied access to the papers relating to the contract. Alderman Fran- comb persists in saying that Councillor Pemberton can see any necessary papers, but the result remains the same—papers and the informa- tion required are not forthcoming on applies- cation. We still think that the Water Committee it as lamentably failed in justifying its position in regard to the present con tract. Alderman Francomb's detailed statement on Tuesday was little more satisfactory than the one he gave a month ago. The details themselves require considerable elucidation which we hope will be forthcoming who* the Local Government Board Inspector holds Ms inquiry. Under the heads of projected expenditure, for instance, there is put down nearly £B,OOO for mains in and out of the borough. Surely these I were or might have been specified in the original scheme for which £147,000 was borrowed? However, an indulgent and not very critical Council accepted the bald explanation vouchsafed, but we are sure there are very many people who will be far from content with the Water Com- mittee's reasons for incurring an additional ex- penditure of £20,510.

Overorowding difficulties have loomed with un- pleasant frequency before the Warrington rate- payers of late and there appears to be every likelihood of another having to be dealt with at no very distant :date. The accommodation at the Padgate Cottage Homes is now, we are told, being severely taxed and there are already lin residence twenty-three more children than thelinstitution is registered for. Nor does the number show any signs of decreasing. On the contrary there are children waiting to be transferred from the Work- house to the Homes. Every-one will be prepared to admit that the schools are doing an admirable work in equipping the rising generation to take their part in the battle of life, and :if they were convinced of the absolute necessity for an extension the ratepayers would, no doubt, acquiesce in the diversion of public funds into a channel which most in the long run save the rates. It would, indeed, b., unfortunate if the excellent system of training were hampered by overcrowded conditions. The fact that another large building scheme is already in hand must not be overlooked, and it therefore behoves the Guardians to pro- ceed with caution and, if possible, to devise means other than building to cope with the situation.

There has been something akin to a musica revival in Warrington of late years. Local talent has come to the fore with refreshing persistence, new organizations have been created, and there is on every hand evidence that the refining and elevating influence:of music is playing a largee part' in the recreation and education of Warrington people. Who, ten years ago, would have seriously thought of an Eisteddfod being successfully engineered in Warrington.? Yet for three years in succession, by dint of the dauntless energy and enthusiasm of a few local artistes, we have bad .a musical festival which has done the town credit. Gratifying is it to know that the Eisteddfod with which the year 1905 opened was, both in point of public support and the high standard oi the competitions,superior to its predecessors. We believe it has been sug- gested that the festival in future years might with advantage, be made a general town under- taking instead of confining the arrangements to the enterprize of the few. The sphere of interest would, no doubt, be enlarged by such a step, but whether the suggestion becomes an accomplished fact or not, Warrington will have cause to be indebted to the lovers of music who first intro- duced the time honoured Welsh institution for the enjoyment and cuyure of the townspeople of War- rington.

The historian of Mr. Gladstone and depositary of his most cherished political confidences is understood to be writing his impressions of the United States. His recent tour was not his first visit, but he is deliberate in all his actions, and the wonder, indeed, will be that he should deliver himself on so near a subject at all. A con- tributor to a current weekly has been endeavouring to analyse Mr. Morley's character, its strength and weakness. A man who was not afraid of a paradox would have aid of Mr. Morley that his strength was his weakness, and a little evidence of weakness would

make his name stronger in the eyes of the people. I Mr. Morley has nothing of the demagogue about him. All our great modern statesmen have had just a suspicion of it—Disraeli and Gladstone con- spicuously; Lord Rosebery and Mr. Balfour in lesser degree. Mr. Morley commands respect I rather than affection; and it is his difficulty of I raising enthusiam that barred his way to being ' Leader of the Liberal party—a position which I seemed his by every qualification. Though he is 4 austere' and appears 0:3 some to be proud, Mr. Moiley has the quality of never under-estimating his opponents. Like the late Sir William Harcourt, too, he kept the friendship of Mr. Chamberlain, all through the political fissures and fusions; and has it still; though nobody has said more caustically what he thinks of Mr. Chamberlain's politics. Yet he it was who induced a gathering at the National Liberal Club to cheer Mr. Chamberlain for his courage and patriotism on that tour through South Africa. One-eyed politicians cannut understand this ; but it is part of Mr. Morley's strength—and weakness!

Something of pathetic interest attaches to an I article in the" Monthly Review" by the Rev. J. M. Bacon on "The Birth of Telegraphy." This well-known aeronaut and scientific writer passed away but a fortnight ago; and in possibly the last article Whose proofs he corrected on finds him including telepathy in his outlook, and inquiring whether our 'scientific " developments of the fu- ture may not depend upon the perfecting of man's faculties rather than upon any mechanical ingenu- ity or material wonder. Strange things, he says, happen on the borderland of the normal. Dr. Alexander Bain's statement is quoted that "in the 1 delirium of fever the sense of hearing sometimes become extraordinarily acute, and that among the premonitory symptoms of brain disease has been I noticed an unusual delicacy of the sense of sight." But the essayist has an answer for the doctor, in that this abnormal acuteness of sense may belong to the most normal conditions. The natives of 1 the St. Lawrence country were told by their medicine man of the coming of the French ,t whites," and set out on a river voyage of many weeks to meet them. General Gordon's death was known in the streets of Cairo on the day of its occurrence. "In like manner it has been con- stantly reported during the present Russo-Japanese War that the Chinese have appeared to be in possession of intelligence which could have been conveyed through no obvious channel." Animals scent danger and seismic trouble ; and it becomes , a question (says the essayist) whether some mode of obtaining intelligence from a, distance may not have been acquired by certain creatures in a state of nature, as also by native tribes, and whether some survival of this may not be found in second sight among civilized peoples.

At Frodsham, on Thursday, a sheep strayed on to the London and North-Western Railway line, and when on the Frodsham Bridge it leaped over a high parapet guarding the River Weaver arches, and, falling a distance of over seventy feet, was killed. St. George's Day School. At the request of the Education Committee the Managers of St. George's Mission have granted an extension of the use of their premises until the• end of July, 1906. The Local Educational Authority will pay rent at the rate of .£1 a week, from April let, 1904, to July 31st, 1906. Local Harriers

Nineteen members of the Warrington AC., turned out for an ordinary pack run from headquarters on Saturday. The distance covered was 6.1 miles, and the northern junior champion, C. J. Straw finished first in 40 minutes, closely followed by tbe Brothers Parsonage. Poor Children's Treat,

The officers and teachers of Wycliffe Cockhedge Mission held their annual treat for poor children on Monday when 320 sat down to tea. Afterwards there was an entertainment, which was greatly enjoyed by the children. The committee desire to thank all who so willingly subscribed for the treat.

FIRE GRATES of every description.—J. S. GAR- LAND, Furnishing Ironmonger, 51, 53, a 55, Bridge- street, Warrington.—Advt. Burning Fatality. Patrick McCormick, aged three years, 5, Bewsey- street, was admitted to the Infirmary yesterday morn- ing suffering from burns. It is supposed that he was trying to light something at the fire when his clothing became ignited. He was severely burned about the body. and died a few hours after adm6sion to the institution. Lancashire Coal Trade. The Lancashire coal trade is just now experiencing a run of exceptional briskness. Same of the pits are winding longer hours in order to keep pace with orders. The delay occasioned by the recent fogs has not yet been made up. Five days per week are being worked at the majority of the pits, some few having resulted to Saturday winding. PUBLIC3BENEFIT. UNDER THE CLOCK FOR BEST BOOTS TN LANCASHIRE.—Advt. Death of Mr. Henry Lawton, of Moore. On Monday afternoon the remains of the late Mr. Henry Lawton,a highly respected inhabitant of Moore, who passed away in his seventy-seventh year, were interred in the Daresbury Church Yard. The Vicar (Rev. F. Clifton Smith) performed the last rites in the presence of a large gathering of relatives and friends. Many floral tributes were forwarded. PUBLIC BENEFIT.—UNDER THE CLOCK FOR BEST BOOTS IN LANCASHIRE.—Advt. Fire Last Night. At 7.30 last night. the Warrington Fire Brigade received information by telegram of an outbreak on a farm at Newton-by-Daresbury. The men turned out promptly with the engine, Major," and up to a late hour had not returned. The fire was evidently a serious one, as the glare could be seen for miles around. UNDER THE CLOCK for Good BooTs.-.-Advt. Sportsman's Unusual ",Bag."

A well-known local sportsman was fortunate in obtaining, yesterday, whilst out shooting, a fine specimen of wild goose of the Brent species. The bird scaled 7-lbs. 2-ozs., and measured 4ft. 2in. across the wings. It is a rarity for wild geese to be in this district, and many local wild fowlers will no doubt feel envious. Old Blues' Re-Union The brightly • decorated rooms at the Warrington Blue Coat school were the scene of a most enjoyable gathering on Thursday evening when the jubilee of the Blue Goat Brotherly Society was celebrated by the holding of a re-union of Old Blues. The attendance numbered about sixty, and included Councillor W. J. and Mrs. Forshaw. Excellent arrangements had been made by a sub-committee consisting of Messrs. F. Short, John Moss, J. Dobson and W. Rutter who had the hearty co operation of Mr. E. Rose, the respected headmaster. The affair was a suc- cess in every way. Dancing was indulged in from eight to twelve, Mr. Lawson supplying the music. Mr. Dobson was the M.C. During the intervals songs were rendered by Mrs. Short and Mr. W. Broughton, and Mr. Alf. Grounds provoked considerable amusement by his humorous sketches Mr. Foster gave a conjuring exhibition. The catering was in the capable hands of Mrs. Atkinson. Mr. E. Rose whs heartily thanked for his valuable services in helping forward the arrangements, on the proposi- tion of Mrs. F. Short.

The Late Mr. John Houghton. The funeral of the late Mr. John Houghton, Heath Lodge, one of the oldest and most highly respected residents of Padgate, who passed away yesterday week took place at the Warrington Cemetery on Monday. The deceased gentleman who in a few days would have attained his 71st birthday I..et with a serious accident some years ago from the effects of which he never recovered. ile was a Conseivative and a Churchman and for years represented the township on the Board of Guardians, being the oldest member of the authority at the time of his retirement The funeral was preceded by a service in Padgate Church. The Rev. W. Pollock-Hill, vicar of Padgate officiated. The choir of Padgate Church sang the hymns, 'Peace, perfect peace" and "..ow the labourer's task is o'er." Mr. Blackwell pregiding at the organ. Local Musical Successes. At the recent examinations of the Incorporated Society of Mlnicians held in Liverpool. Winifred Littler passed in Grade 111, and Joanna Murray in Preparatory Grade in pianoforte playing. Both are pupils of Miss Margaret Chisholm L.R.A..11., at Beaconfield School, Weston. Footballers Robbed. A robbery from Shugars and J. Davies well-known members of the Warrington Football club, is reported. It appears that both lodge at a house in Old-road and on Monday Shugars was away at Bradfore. whilst D ivies went to Wigan. On returning, home at night Shugars discovered that £2B of his had disappeared from a portmanteau in his bedroom, while Davies missed £5. The police have the matter in hand. Depression in the Cheshire Cheese Trade. Mr. R. Challinor (the secretary to the Cheshire Dairy Farmers' Association) states that the cheese trade of 1901 had not been so depressed since 1879. In comparing figures with 1903 he estimated that cheesemakers had lost £105,250. The quality of the cheese was by no means lower, than that of previous years : on the other hand, experts stated that the reputation of , Cheshire cheese never stood higher than it did now.l In his (Mr. Challinor's) opinion the cause of the depression was the general depression of trade. Other causes undoubtedly affected the consumption, such as the increased impartation of the foreign com- petitor's product, with its distribution at unremunera- tive prices. Baptist Sick and Burial Society. The annual " divide "in connection with the Hill Cliffe Baptist Sunday School Sick and Burial Society was held last week when the officers and a number of teachers, parents, and scholars met. The President, the Rev. J. S. Hughes, presided. The Treasurer (Mr. Joseph Moss, jun.,) stated that the funds were in a healthy state, and that notwithstanding the exception- al drain upon the club during the year, through the death of two members, an experience which had only happened once before in twelve years, they had over £2O in hand, which would enable them to pay three and tenpence to each mninber and leave a substantial balance in hand. This was agreed to. Thanks were accorded to the officers, on the motion of Mr. Phillips and Mrs. Cliff e. Th.,3 officers were re-appointed. Police Court and Prim Gate Mission. The following are some of the items in the work of the Police Court and Prison Gate Mission of the Liverpool Diocesan C.E.T.S. for the months of Decem- ber. The members of the staff made 252 visits to 26 police and petty sessional courts, and conversed with 1,195 persons in the courts and cells of whom 543 were afterwards visited in their abodes. The magistrates enlisted the help of the missionaries on -bah all of 56 persons in the courts, and the court officials and the police had their assistance for 42 persons in the cells and courts ; 39 women, girls and boys were taken to homes, sent back to friends, or provided with employment ; 28 tickets for work at the Society's Firewood and Mat Factory were given to men.

Warrington Ragged School Mission. On Sunday 300 mince pies and 600 oranges were distributed to the children attending the Mission. On Monday evening the annual teachers tea meeting took place followed by a discourse on the work of the Mission, snd some suggested improvements. Messrs. Skelland, Owen, Gough, O'dell, Miss Monks and other workers took Fart in the discussion, Mr. Skelland also related his eKperiences of Sunday School work. The officials are helping, as well as their funds will allow deserving cases of distress among the poor, A number of tickets have been given out entitling the recipients to doles of bread &c. Donations are thank- fully acknowledged from : Mr. Carter, 200 buns ; Mrs. Smith (baker), 501bs of bread, a parcel of cloth bag from Miss Gill, Wilderspool-road. There are many really dPserviug cases in the town and to help them, the management appeal for donations of money, food and clothing.

r' Big Farm Fire. , , During the whole of Sunday the Warrington and Winwick Asylum Fire Brigades were engaged in sub- duing a disastrous outbreak at the farm of Mr. Peter Longton, Winwick. It was about ten o'clock when the alarm was raised and the Warrington Brigade under Deputy Superintendent Turner were soon upon the scene. They found that the flames had got a firm hold on a Dutch barn, five bays long, built of wood and corrugated iron and containing stacks of hay, .corn and straw,sacks of corn, farm implements, carts, and a thrashing machine and an engine belonging to Mr. Bond, of Glazebrook. Fortunately the Winwick Asylum Brigade, in charge of Captain Parker, were on the spot before the arrival of the Warrington men and they had rendered valuable service, the united brigades ultimately succeeding in saving a stack and the remainder of the farm buildings. The damage is •estimated at about £l,OOO. The origin of the fire is a mystery. 1, Technical Institute Soiree.

A highly successful and enjoyable gathering was the fifth annual students' soiree in connection with the Municipal Technical Institute which was held in the Parr Hall, on Tuesday evening. The assembly numbered abcrit 250, and dancing was indulged in with spirit until the early hours of the morning, an excellent selection of music being supplied by Mr. A Starkey's band. Mr. J. Moore Murray efficiently dis •charged the duties of master of , and the following stewards lent valuable assistance : —Messrs. E. Haigh, H, Whittle, E, A. Clayton, I. Pharoah. H. Boyle and F. Gartland. An admirable secretary was found in Mr. E. Haigh who worked most assiduously 'for the success of the function. Daring the evening the Mayor and Mayoress and several members of the Education Committee were in attendance. The catering was in the hands of Mrs. Atkinson and gave complete satisfaction. 'Coroner Thanks Jurymen. Mr. S. Brighouse, county coroner, took the oppor- tunity at St. Helens Town Hall, on Monday, of publicly Placing on record his appreciation of the willing and able work of the jurors of South-West Lancashire. -, Mr. Brighouse told the jury that he was sorry to have • 1 to interfere with their holiday, but the business of the t, county had to go on. He honestly offered his best wishes to the jurors of the county, and hoped they would experience a happy and prosperous New Year. Them was a discussion just now going on in some Papers as to the value of jurors, and a judge at assize had expressed his dissatisfaction with nearly all the verdicts that had been returned by the jurors at that assize. He could not express any opinion about civil and criminal cases, but in regard to that court he • ' would be very sorry indeed to take the responsibility of the work unless he was assisted by jurors. In --. many cases he had no technical, or scientific skill that , enabled him to deal with them, but he found more often that the jurors were able to supply that " deficiency. He did not say that as flattering, but he , really found that it would be quite impossible to do the work as conscientiously as he desired unless he - had the assistance of jurors. He also thanked the Police of South-West Lancashire for the very able way in which they got information for him and reported various cases. He also expressed his indebtedness to the Press for their services in record Ing so intelligently the proceedings of the court. The Late Mr. Henry Lee. On Saturday the remains of the late Mr. Henry ,Len (father of Mrs. Ziba Armitage and Mr. Arthur H. -Lee, Warrington) were interred in the Salford Cemetery, Weaste A service at the Broughton Park Congregational Church was conducted by the Rev. Samuel Pearson, pastor of the church. Dr. Henry Watson was at the organ. Two well-known hymns— " There is a land of pure delight " and" Give me the Wings of faith to rise '—were sung by the choir and congregation. After the reading of the Scriptures Mr. Pearson gave an address, in which he spoke of the fine character of the late Mr. Lee, and of his long devotion to good causes. None of his talents had been buried or unused. He was diligent and unselfish in his -use of them all, and he had left the world richer for his presence and now poorer by his absence.— At the close of the service the Dean of Manchester (Dr. Maclure) pronounced the benediction. The body was then borne to the cemetery, where the final prayers were offered by the Rev. Dr. Goodrich. The arrival of the funeral party at the cemetery was awaited by a number of tho prefects of the Grammar School (Mr. Lee was deputy chairman of the Board of Governnrs). and they lined the pathway on each side as the body was taken to the graveside. The Corporation and magistracy of Manchester, as well as numerous organizations xvith which the deceased gentleman had been connected, were represented. The late Mr. Lee was well known at Wycliffe Congrega- tional Church where he had in times past, been an 'occasional worshipper. • PPR GLASS AND CHINA of every description try J. S.
