OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Wycliffe Church. At this church on Sunday morning the annual new year's young people's service was held and was largely attended. In the absence of the Pastor (Rev. John Yonge), through the indisposition of his wife, the service was conducted by Kr: J. A. Yonge (principal of the Congregational school, Saltburn), who delivered an address and distributed Bibles to the members of the Pastor's Catechumen class. In the evening Mr. A. Conley preached. Buckley-street P.S.A. Society. Mr. P. Green presided at an open meeting of the Buckley-street P.S.A. Society held on Sunday in the Methodist Free Church. There was a good attendance of members and friends. The chairman delivered an address on the words "Behold, I stand at the door and knock," and the speaker, Mr. El. Thornton, took for his subject, "He thanked God and took conrage." Miss L. Fairhurst and Miss L. Woodland sweetly rendered the solos.', "He shall feed his flock" and "Come unto Me," while Miss Fairhurst sang "He was despised." Mr. M. Shaw, and Mr. EL Woolley also took part in the service. Miss Plinston acted as accompanist. Re-opening of St. Hilda's Mission; A special service was held on Thursday evening to celebrate the re-opening of this mission which has been closed for nearly two years owing to the fact that there was no assistant curate working in the parish of St. James until the arrival of the Rev. D. R. Davies. The building has undergone considerable renovation and it is hoped that it will quickly become a centre of religions activity in the neighbourhood. There was a good con- gregation at the re-opening service which was conducted by the Rev. H. B. Firth (vicar), assisted by the Rev. D. R. Davies. The preacher was the Rev. H. V. Pigot, vicar of Stockton Heath, who based an appropriate sermon on the words "They continued steadfastly in the Apostle's doctrine and fellowship and in the break- ing of bread and in prayers" Acts of Apostles ii., 42.

EX-BANK MANAGER'S AFFAIRS. Bankruptcy Proceedings at Warrington.

At the Warrington Bankruptcy Court, yesterday, before Nlr. Registrar Ridgway, Thomas Cross Haddock, now residing and carrying on business as an insurance agent at Rose Cottage, Knutsford-road, and formerly branch bank manager at Runcorn, appeared for his public examination. The liabilities amounted to .£l6B 14s. 7d., and the assets consisted of a metal watch valued at 2s. and six sporting prints valued at £3, the deficiency being £165 125. 7d. The debtor gave as the causes of his failurg• :—" 111-health of myself, and the consequent loss of my employment as a Bank Manager; and losses by speculation on the Stock Exchange." The Official Receiver made the following observations on the case :—Receiving Order on Bankrupt's own petition. Two judgments unsatisfied at 'date thereof. Bankrupt states that, from about 1870 to March 1900, he was a bank employee, and for the last five years of that period acted as a manager at Runcorn: that both before and since March, 1900, he has acted as agent for several insurance companies ; not having kept any books of account, or at any time ascertained his financial position, cannot file prescribed defici- ency account, but attributes his deficiency, ,in general terms, to " losses by speculation on the Stock Exchange"; first became aware of his insolvency "about 3 years ago" ; has since contracted some of his exist- ing liabilities ; before his marriage in September, 1888, settled a policy on his own life for .L4OO, and his house- hold furniture on his wife and children, with certain reservations in favour of himself ; and that his wife is possessed of shares in a limited company, a mortgage to secure £650, and an income of about £65 a year arising from other sources. Unsecured liabilities are for :—Bank overdraft, money borrowed, and costs, £5l 16s. 9d. ; clothing, £7l 13s. 3d. ; medical attendance, £25 ' • wines and spirits, £7 17s. 10d.; fixtures, £5 ; and sundries, £7 6s. ad. Mr. W. E. Fowler appeared for the debtor. Mr. Johnson, the assistant official receiver;having ex- amined the debtor at some length, the examination was closed.

PADGATE COTTAGE HOMES. Distribution of riz?.s.

ANOTHER ACCOMMODATION DIFFICULTY. -- A large number of Warrington ladies and gentlemen visited the Padgate Cottage Homes on Thursday even- on the occasion of the annual distribation of prizes by*Miss Broadbent. The function, which took place in the brightly-decorated schoolroom proved highly enjoy- able and successful. Mr. George Mather, (chairman of the Schools Committee) presided, and the attendance included :—Mrs. Clay, Miss Broadbent, Mrs. Wilkinson, the Rev. Dr. Mercer, the Rev. J. Pollock-Hill, Messrs. S. Davies, A. J. Jolley, J. Payton, W. Hickman, R. Forshaw, E. A. Jones, H. Houk, R. Shearer, Mr. and Mrs. J. D. Buckton, Dr. and Mrs. Peacocke, Councillor Wilson, Mr. and Mrs. Wright, andthe staff of the school. The excellent entertainment was submitted and re- flected the greatest credit upon the children and the staff. There were songs, recitations, and drills by th boys and girls, and Mr. West, the clerk to the snperine tendent, under the alias of Professor Blacklake gave a parody of a ventriloquial sketch. Another interesting item was a comical sketch representing a waxwork show in which the principal characters were Mr. West, Mr. Jones, the shoemaker, and Master Postlewaite, Miss Broadbent presented the prizes, giving suitable words of advice and encouragement to each recipient. In responding to a vote of thanks the Chairman made a sympathetic allusion to the death of Mr. John Houghton, a former member of the Board. He read a letter of apology for non-attendance from Mr. J. Billinge, who wrote :—One regrets that your Homes cannot possibly accommodate any more of these unfortu- nate children. Seeing we have now at the Home at Warrington some 20 children awaiting admission to your Homes and as a consequence being deprived of the many advantages of the very excellent home life and tuition so essential to the young, one can only hope that your committee will at an early date make some provision to meet the requirements in some way best known to them. Mr. Mather remarked at the present time there were 223 children in that in- stitution, and they should according to law only have 200, so that they had 23 more than they ought to have. They would have to face the difficulty as Mr. Billinge said in one way or another. The number of children had been increasing steadily he believed, ever since the schools were built. Although the rates were very high. he did not think any ratepayer who had be'?n through the schools would deny that good work hid been done at a very moderate cost.

WEEKLY REPORT ON THE HEALTH

OF WARRINGTON.

WEEK ENDING DECEMBER 31th, 1901. During the week, the following births and deaths were registered:—Warrington Registration District births, 29, deaths, 28. The excess of births over deaths was 1. The birth-rato was equal 22.0. The death-rate from all causes was equal to 21.3 per 1,000 against 19.5 the average of the previous 4 weeks and against 19•6 the corresponding period of last year. The death rate from the seven chief zymotic diseases was equal to nil per 1,000, against I'6 the average rate of the previous 4 weeks and against nil the corresponding period of 1903. The ages of mortality were:- 0-1 1-5 5-15 15-25 25-65 65—up. Total 7 3 1 1 13 3 28 The deaths included-1 from phthisis, 11 from lung diseases, 3 from accidents, 1 from suicide, 12 from all other causes. The following cases of infectious disease have been reported during .the wees :—Scarlet fever, 8 cas'es ; diphtheria, 1 case; enteric fever, 2 cases; erysipelas, 3 cases. Total number of patients in hospital, week ending 31th December, 75. METEOROLOGY.—The mean temperature was 39 Fah. and therefore 8 degrees higher than the preceding. week The maximum temperature 52 F., was recorded on the 29th inst., and the minimum temperature 30 V., on the 25th inst. The rainfall measured 007 inch. The general direc- tion was S.W. J. GUEST GORNAT.T., Medical Officer of Health.

suspicions. To come directly to the matter under con- sideration they must see first who ordered the reports. There seemed no doubt the reports were ordered by the Commissioners directly or through their agent. Now who had power to order it? The powers of the Com- missioners contained nothing to entitle them or any- body else to pledge the credit of Warrington for printing. They merely had to report to the Council and the Non-Trading Association, and in ordering the print- ing they had exceeded their powers. But why, also, did they go in for such an expensive sort of printing, with its foolscap size? He thought it was altogether un- necessary to go to such a tremendous expense. Why was the printing given to the " Examiner "and then sent out of town after; and why was it not tendered for? The bill should be paid.,, After the attacks that had been made by small-minded people that night, did the ratepayers wonder that a lot of respectable people would not go on the Council? The MAYOR: In fact, if you keep on there will be no respectable people in it. (Hear, hear.) Councillor WILKINSON: lam thinking about leav- ing. (Laughter.) In answe • to Councillor Stott, The MA.vou said this was an ordinary business trans- action. Messrs. Walker had sent in an account and it had been refused to be paid. They could go to the County Court to enforce payment if they wished. That was the position of affairs, and all this twaddle they had been listening to was not worth spending five minutes moon. (Hear, hear and laughter.) The minutes were then approved.

THE NON-TRADERS' REPORT.

COUNCIL AND THE DISPUTED PRINTING ACCOUNT. --- Refusal to Pay.

There was a long and rather heated discussion at the meeting of the Town Council on Tuesday evening, res- peating a resolution of the General Purposes Committee that an account sub;nitted by Messrs. John Walker and Company, of £22 for the printing of 150 copies of the Non-Trading Commissioners' report, be not paid. The DlA.voit having formally moved the adoption of the minutes the Town Clerk, at tie request of the Conncil,read the following report which had been pre- pared by the Deputy Town clerk : Town Hall, Warrington, December, 1904. The follow- ing are the p trticulars in connection with the printing of the Non-Trader's Report.—On Thursday morning,the 20th October last, a clerk came into my office and said that Mr. Dodds wished to speak to me at the telephone. I went to the instrument and Mr. Dodds said : " Our report is ready ; we have been working on it nearlyall la-st night and it will have to be printed." He also said that he had had a great deal to do with the report. He has never made any secret of this fact. I said that if he would let me have the report I would take it to the "Guardian " Office and let him have a proof as soon as (possible. He then said: " I have given it to the Examiner' Office, as Walker,as you probably know,of ten comes up to my house." I replied that it was rather awkward, as the" Guardian" Mee had our printing contract and the work ought to have gone there. Mr. Dodds then asked if I would see Dir. Walker' and arrange with him, as ha thought the Corporation should pay for the printing, otherwise the cost would fall on him, and that he had already ptid a considerable sum in connection with the movement. I agreee that it would have to be printed and that I would see Dir. Walker. I immediately went to the " Examiner " Office. Mr. Walker was not in. I saw the foreman, who told me they were printing the report. I arranged to call again about noon, which I did. I wts then told that Hr. Walker had taken the report to Manchester to be printed, and that it could not be done in \Warrington in the time. Dir. Walker left word for ma to call at three o'clock. I subsequently ascertained by telephone that Mr. Walker had returned, and I immediately went to see him, and asked him to glee me a price for the printing. He stated that he could not do so, as it was being printed by a Manchester firm, and that it had to be got out at once. consequently it would mean a lot of night work. Ele also stated that it was being set up foolscap size,and that he would have twelve nulls of the proof in the morning. I explained that -foolscap size would not do for the Corporation, as it should be uniform in size with the Council Summons. I also told him that I could not consent to give him a blank cheque to charge what he liked for the printing, and that unless it was done Council Summons size I should not have anything to do with it, and that he could take it the Corporation would not pay for the printing, as the whole ciritunstances in connection with it were most irregicPar and improper. (Hear hear.) The matter was left at this until 10 o'clock the following morning (Friday), when I arranged to see him again. I then communicated the above facts to the three Council Commissioners. I went to the "Examiner" Office the next morning about 10.30 and was told that Mr. Walker was engaged in his private office going through the proofs with Messrs. Williams, Pemberton, and Dodds. I called again at 12 o'clock and was told that they were still engaged. I called again a few minutes after one o'clock and was told that the Com- missioners had just left, and that the;revised proofs had been sent to Manchester to be printed off. I did not see Mr. Walker until after two o'clock when he told me that the Commissioners wanted 200 copies, as they were sending a copy of the report all over the country, and that he would have to wire at once if any additional copies were required by the Corporation. I pressed him to name a price, but he would not do so. f.:20 was mentioued as the cost of a similar job. I then distinctly stated that the Corporation would not pay any such stain, and that if he liked I would agree to the printing of 150 copies-100 to be sent to the Mayor and 50 to the Non-Traders' Commissioners at a fair price to be afterwards agreed on. I further explained about the " Guardian" office having our contract Dir. Walker agreed to my terms, and gave me a revised proof of the report, which I had at once set up in type at the "Guardian" Office, and received a proof in less than four hours. (Hear, hear.) I had previously the same day obtained from Mr. Coancillor Wilkinson an incomplete copy of the Council Commissioners' report. and had that set up in type. I also telephoned to Mr. Dodds and asked him if he would send a copy of the Council Commissioners report with the Non-Traders' report which I understood he was sending out to various newspapers that night. He stated that he could not consent without consulting the Commissioners. The reports were received at the "Examiner" Ornce at nine o'clock, and 100 sALto the Corporation. I have since ascertained that OEM 23 10s. charged to the Corporation represents the cost of printing the whole 300 copies of the report, of which the Corporation only received 100. 'lam sill of the opinion that the workicould have been done in Warrington in less time and considerably cheaper.—Yours truly, STANLEY WiLsofc. The MAYOR said that what he understood would have been done was that when the report was written a copy should have been sent to him as Mayor of the Borough and representative of the Council. He should have given instructions for copies to be printed and oneseut to each member of the Council. Any other way of doing the business was irregular. He could not, how- ever, say much about the matter. He had not been called upon to adjudicate, for the matter was now left entirely in the bands of the Council. Councillor STOTT asked the Town Clerk if the Com- missioners had the power to present the report in any form they liked? Had they the power to have it set up and then present it? The MAYOR said it was not treating him with proper courtesy not to send him the report, considering that the Council had paid for all the expenses of the Corn• missioners, all the shorthand notes and everything else. When he as Mayor had read the report and communi- cated it to the Council, the Non-Traders could have sent it all over the country. The Council Commissioners sent their report to him first. Councillor STOTT : Had the three Non-Trading Com- missioners power under the condition ot the inquiry to present the report printed. The MAYOR: There was no hard and fast line laid down. The matter was left to the general honesty and straightforwardness of the whole of the Commissioners. Councillor WILKINSON: They N,tere asked to send in

a report. The Mavon: We asked them to send in a report. The T0W1.7 CLERK: The proper course would have been to send the manuscript report to the Mayor. as the other Commissioners did. Alderman HETCFIINSON: Is it a fact that copies of the report were sent all over the country before the Mayor received it. The TOWN CLERK: I have ascertained that two hundred copies were sent out the same night that the report was sent to the Mayor. Alderman BENNETT contended that if it was a fact that the report was sent out without the report of the other Commissioners to the papers it was little less than an outrage. He had always felt that their Non-Trading frieuds however good their motives might have ibeen, had very little regard for the honour of the town, and if they had wanted to give Warrington a bad nam6 and attach an odium to it that was likely to stick, they could not have adopted a better method than the send- , ing out of this one-sided version of the findings of the I Commissioners. (Hear, hear.) The MaYon : I would not say anything about that. We will take it for granted that they were acting in a bona-fide and straightforward manner. Alderman BENNEtr, continuing, said that in one of the newspapers Warrington was called a demoralized municipality, and he could only come to the conclusion that the man who wrote the article had only seen one report. That might have occurred in many papers: and as one who was proud of Warrington, in spite of all its faults, he felt the matter f•ery iceenly and thought that what was done was extremely unfair. There was no paper in England that would have written an article like the one he had referred to if it had been familiar with all the facts. Obviously the judgment was formed on the Non-Trading report only, and Warring- ton was held up as a demoralized municipality. Of course, it was nothing of the sort, but the people all over Lancashire and England did not know that and a stigma was attached to the town which they were all anxious to remove. (Hear, hear.) He thought the most ordinary sense of courtesy. of real loyalty to the town, and real local patriotism, would have prevented the Commissioners acting in that one-sided manner. Councillor TINNION said one reason why he would like to say something about this matter was' in the iaterest of the Deputy Town Clerk. They had two versions of what transpired before them. One was con- tained in the" Examiner' and the other was given by Mr. Wilson. He took it that even the Non-Traders took no objection to anything Mr. Wilson hitherto lied done, as they had said he was the one white hen among the officials of the Corporation who never laid astray, although they had found fault with almost every other official. Mr, Wilson was always anxious to do what he possibly could, and was at the Commissioners' elbows every day and sometimes night as well—(laughter)--- and was an ollicial in whom the Non-Traders had confi- dence. The Council, he took, it wasiprepared to accept Wilson's version as absolutely true. (Hear' hear.) He (Councillor Tinnion) passed over Mr. Dodds, I because he was simply acting as the agent of the Commissioners,who were reTponsible.lie had had, they were told a great deal to do with the report, and never made any secret of the fact. He (Councillor Tinnion) had had something to do with the reports on this question. He remembered one report being issued— with all due respect to the others, the only sensible re- . port—which was termed a whitewashing report. Well, for pure unadulterated whitewashing commend him to • a portion of the Non-Traders' report ! (Laughter and hear, hear.) It excelled everything he ever came across. EMMANUEL SCHOOL TEA MEETING.

New School Premises Secured.

The gratifying announcement that new school pre- mises had been secured was made at the annual tea party in connection with Emmanuel Reformed Church which was held in the Schoolroom over Arpley Station on Thursday evening. The affair was a distinct success, the brightly decorated room being filled with a gathering of about 120 people. The following ladies presided at the tables : Mrs. Vaughan, Mrs. Norman, , Miss Lees, Mrs. R. Lees, Mrs. Armstrong, Miss Allen, Mrs. William Dickens, Ars. A. Dickens and Mrs, Elias Allen. Tea was follovvel by an entertainment, the Pastor (Rev. F. Vaughan) preaiding. A very interest- ing programme was submitted, the cho:r rendering a glee, the part song " Silent night " and the anthem "In Thee 0 Lord, do I put my trust." Songs were effectively given by Miss Armstrong, Mr. L. Aukland, and Mr. Clayton, and there were enjoyable quartettes by Messrs. Clayton, Lloyd, Dnsworth and Bate, and Mr. Grounds and party. Mr. Peterson gave a cornet solo in capital style. airs. A. Landon discharged the duties of accompanist. In the course of an address, the PASTOR said it was just about twelve months since he received the invita- tion of the vestry to accept the pastorate of that church. Neither Mrs. Vaughan nor himself had had one single cause to regret the step they took in coming to labour for God in that part of His kingdom. When he looked into the past history of the church and saw what spirit-filled men had been privileged to labour there, and what great work had been done, he felt some- what tremulous in taking up the pastorate. Whatever success might have attended their movement had been largely due to the loyal help he had received from the wardens (Mr. Thomas Allen and Mr. Wm. Dickens) and the congregation. Not only for their own circle, but from the ministerial body in the town had he received encouragement, and that augured well for the future and gave them great comfort and cause for thankfulness. A fellow-minister had told him that it was absolutely impossible to stir up religious enthu- siasm in Warrington,but he was hoping and praying that they at Emmanuel Church would be able to prove by their work that that was not true. They would be pleased to hear that Mr. William Miner& had been licensed by the Bishop as lay-reader at the:church. The old Bible class had developed into a men's Union which was now a force to be reckoned with, for there was an enrolled membership of between seventy and eighty. The renovation of the exterior of the church was carried out under the direction and at the expense of the members of that organization. During the year they had sent out the first ordained missionary of their church to Tndia, in the person of Mr. Callon. That was the last occasion on which they would hold their tea meetings in that room which had served them for church purposes during the past seventeen years. The lease!had expired and the Railway Company required the premises. He was pleased to be able to inform the meeting that they were now in possession of a site and building absolutely their own, for negotiations had just been completed, whereby they acquired the old school of St. James's in Knutsford-road. (Applause.) Considerable funds would be required and he invited any member of the gathering to signify his or her willing- ness to be responsible for the collection of a certain amount. Guarantees to the extent of £73 were immediately forthcoming and the amount was further increased during the evening.

Warrington Steamer's Salvage. There has been a peculiar case of salvage by the Warrington steamer, Gertrude, belonging to Messrs. Joe. Monks and Co. Ltd., this week. Two vessels, the Kathleen and Stromboli, recently collided and sunk in the Clyde just off Greenock. On Monday, the small coasting steamer Flelen ran on to the wreck of the Kathleen and was held fast until help came from the Gertrude, which was inward bound under the command of Captain John S. Monks. The War- rington steamer towed the Helen off the wreck, and her owners have since lodged a claim in the Scotch Courts for salvage.

THE TRAM FARES TO STOCKTON HEATH.

CORPORATION DECIDE TO CHARGE lid. "Cussedness " of Stockton Heath People.

A considerable portion of the time occupied by the Town Council at their monthly meeting on Tuesday, was devoted to the question of the fare to be charged for the completed tram route to Victoria-square, and after considerable discussion it was decided that the fare shall be one half-penny from Stafford-road to Victoria-square. The recommendation that this should be the fare was contained in the minutes of the Electricity and Tramways Committee. Alderman SNI ETHURST, in proposing the adoption of the minutes said he had no difficulty in putting the recommendation to the Council as at the meeting of the committee it was approved—only two voting against it.

Councilor WILKINSON seconded. Councilloi P. PEACOCK moved that the resolution be referred back and also that a recommendation that children should be charged one penny for the whole of the distance, be similarly dealt with. The length of the L atchford route be pointed out was 2,730 yards or 240 yards longer than the Wilderspool route would be when it was completed. He therefore failed to see upon what grounds they could ask the people of Stockton Heath and Warrington residents travelling out to Stockton Heath to pay 132 d. for a distance so much less than the Latchford route. He hoped the committee would re- consider the matter as be did not think their present policy a good one. They looked to the Stockton Heath people for any profit which they hoped to make and therefore he did not think it wise that they should adopt a prohibitive charge. Councillor STOTT seconded the amendment and ex- pressed the hope that the majority of the Council would vote for it. It appeared to him that to charge lid. would be an injustice not only to Stockton Heath people but also to VArrington people making use of the service. Moreover it would be about the dearest tram ride under any municipality in the kingdom. He did not think users of the route would pay lid. and in all pro- bability if the extra charge was insisted upon they would have fewer passengers travelling the extra dis- tance than there were travelling on the Longford. route at the present time. Therefore from a financial stand- point and f rom the standpoint of the Corporation and the ratepayers in general bethought it would be advisable to reconsider the matter and make tbe charge one penny for the whole distance.

Councillor TisxroB thought the amendment was moved on very slight grounds as the only reason the proposer could give was that the route was so much shorter than another route in the town. Strictly speaking they were not dealing with a tramway extension. When they got the tramway order to Stafford-road they felt that it would be to the advantage of the people in Stocipton Heath and also to the owners of the cars if the Cor- poration could carry the service to Victoria Square. They therefore had to obtain a light railway order and it seemed to him to be nothing but stupidity for anyone to expect that they should spend the extra money in getting that order, and get nothing more in return. With regard to the state- ment that they would carry fewer passengers if the extra halfpenny were charged, he did not see how they I could be any the worse off than they were at present, as they would only continue to carry the passengers they carried to-day if they charged one penny for the whole of the distance. There were some sensible people in Warrington, if there were none outside, who would pay lid. to get to Victoria-square. Teey surely could not expect that the ratepayers of Warrington were going to spend £9,000 to carry the light railway from the swing bridge to Victoria-square without getting I some additional revenue. The work was in the interests of people in Stocktoa Heath, many of whom no doubt had very valuable interests in Warrington, and many of whom had no interest whatever in the town, and he as a representative of the ratepayers was not prepared to advised that they should consider only the interests of those people who were outside the borough. The addi- tional portion of the route had been most costly— working out at £32.000 per mile, whereas the general rate per mile in Warrington was about £B,OOO. He did not think the people of Stockton Heath deserved any consideration at their bands, as they had not shown any consideration to the Corpora- tion. The cost of the small extra length had been enormous simply because the people of Stockton Heath had had their pound 01,t flesh and therefore—he did not say it in any vindictin spirit—he did not think they shoed consider the people of Stockton Heath. To him it appeared to be only a reasonable business proposal that if the Corporation had to spend £9,000 they should get something in return for it. He took it that the persons who already spent a penny to ride from Stafford- road to the Swing Bridge would continue to do so and therefore the revenue of the Corporation would be increased if lid. were charged, to the extent of the extra number of persons who would travel from the Swing Bridge to Victoria Square. Moreover if a charge of one penny was decided upon the committee would not be able to alter the charge whereas if lid. were charged the fare could easily be reduced if the committee found the route did not pay. The proposal was carried unani- monsly in committee and he thought the committee were the best judges of what was in the interests of the tramways and of the ratepayers. Councillor MAGINN said that as a member of the Tramways Committee he was not present when the question was considered, but he thoroughly agreed with the decision of the commitee. He said now as he always had stated that it was a wild cat scheme from ' beginning to end to expect Warrington to carry their tramways out of the borough before their system covered the whole of the town, and yet they were carrying their system into Stockton Heath in spite of the cussedness of the people there, (Laughter.) Alderman SMETHURST : Blessedness you should say. (Laughter.) Councillor MAGINN continuing said some years ago it was tried to include Stockton Heath in the borough and if they went to-morrow and asked the people of Stockton Heath to come inte the borough and have the benefit of the high rates their answer would remain the same and would be written in letters which could be read a mile off. Yet the Stockton Heath people asked that they should be given a train route for nothing. He did not blame them for asking that as if he were in their place he would be just as greedy He could understand the people of Stockton Heath wanting to have the trams for nothing but what he could not understand was that members of the Town Council and ratepayers, or supposed ratepayers, should want to spend the ratepayers money on Stockton Heath. The cost of running the cars was 9d. per mile and they could only expect an extra revenue for the increased cost of running the cars to Victoria-square by charging an extra fare. (Hear, hear.) He was, astonished that. gentlemen whom he presumed had a good feeling towards the town, from some wild scheme of seeking a name should advocate the spending of money outside the borough. The alavort, said the members of the Corporation must look at this matter from a business point of view. Would the people of Stockton Heath lose anything by being charged an extra half-penny? He did not think they would. It should be borne in mind that the Corporation had spent £9,000 in widening the road and laying the tram lines and crossing every peculiar bridge He thought the committeehad acted very wisely indeed in first trying to see what the revenue would be before they decided to charge a penny only. At present their income from the Wilderspool route was 15d. per mile. The opening of the new line would mean extra cost and the question was were they prepared to incur that extra expenditure without seeking an extra revenue? (Hear, hear). Councillor LORD said he was one of the two in com- mittee who opposed the resolution for lid. fares. It was never put to the unanimous vote. Alderman SMETHURST : I said: " Now, gentlemen, let us have a unanimous vote. Those in favour, say "ave." The resolution was unanimously carried so far as.l know. Councillor LORD : I differ from yon. The MAYOR : That is not of any consequence. Councillor LORD: It would have made my position here very inconsistent, because T intended to oppose the resolution in the Council, and I shall do so. (Inter- ruption). Councillor Witicrssaa : He is talking of consistency Councillor LORD continuing pointed out that the tramways manager had advised the committee to adopt a penny fare and if anyone should have any knowledge of the question it was their tramways manager. He supported the amendment and he thought it was in the best interests of the running of the trams that one penny should be charged. Councillor BARTON said he was sorry the Corporation ever contemplated the crossing of the bridge. (Hear, hear.) He thought the people In Stockton Heath were doing very well seeing they did not contribute to the rates. He thought the money spent on the light rail- way should have been spent in extending the tramways to Sankey Green and Lovely-lane. (Hear, hear.) In reply to Councillor WILKINSON the Town Clerk explained that had not the Corporation obtained the light railway order to Victoria-square the power would have been obtained by the Northwich Light Rsilway promoters and in that event the Corporation would not have had the power to run a circular route should they contemplate taking such a step. Councillor WILKINSON thought it only right that the Corporation should expect extra revenue for the expen- diture of extra capital. If the extra fare was not a success there7would be no harm in re-considering the question. Alderman SMETHURST vplving to the discussion said he felt confident that the motion for a lid, fare would be carried by a great majority. He was surprised to find gentlemen opposing a committee which had been sitting for so many years considprlng this kind of thing and giving their best time and judgment. There were

gentlemen who were not on the committee,and who had taken no particular interest in the matter, jumping up and proposing amendments. There were two gentlemen who were the most extravagant members of the Council. (Cries of" Name.") Vell, Councillors Peacock and Stott who wanted nothing but bowling greens and baths put in every ward. (Laughter). One of those gentlemen actually opposed the decision of the Council come to only after very careful 'consideration, with re- gard to halfpenny fares on the Longford route. If they were to be guided by the advice of a few of the members of the Council they would have the rates up to 10s. in the .£ in a few years. (Councillor PEMBERTON: They will be.) If they allowed the people on the Wilderspool route to ride for a penny they would have the Latchford people wanting to ride for a halfpenny as ths route was shorter. (Councillor LORD: It is longer.) If the route did not pay where would the deficit come from? (A Votex : "The rates.") The money would come from the rates and they could not levy a rate upon the Stockton Heath people. He asked the Council to show their disapproval of the action of several erroneous members of the Council who jumped up against a committee of 14—(Councillor LORD: Only six voted)—and asked them to sacrifice a halfpenny fare. He asked the Council to give a unanimous Tote and let the people see they were uot to be "jumbled" with such nonsensical proposals. (Hear, hear). On a vote being taken six voted for and 21 against the amendment which was consequently lost. The voting was: For the amendment, Councillors P. Peacock, Lord, Pemberton, Stott. Wright, and Horrocks: against, Aldermen Smethurst, Bennett, Hutchinson and Evans, Councillors Tinnion, Forshaw, Wilkinson, Roberts, Ashcroft, Clegg, Barton, Skelland, Magian, Wilson, Sankey, Burton, Craik, Fulton Smith, Ellison and Hulse and the Mayor.

HEAVY LOCOMOTIVES. Councillor TiNsioN, in moving the adoption of the minutes of the Paving and Sewerage Committee, stated that Messrs. With regard to the wharf adjoining the storeyard, it was not of much use at present, but it was hoped that when the river was further i-nproved it would be largely used. Councillor ROBEgTS seconded the adoption of the minutes, which were agreed to. MR. GREENING'S GIFT. At a meeting of the Museum Committee a letter was read from Mr. Lintuwas Greening offering to present to the Museum his natural history collection, and it was resolved that cordial thanks be given to him for his generous donation. It was also resolved that Mr. Green- ing be appointed a member of the committee. -.Alderman BENNETT, moving the adoption of the minutes, referred in warm terms to Mr. Greening to whom, he said, their best thhnks were due for his hand- some present. For years past they had been indebted to Mr. Greening for much hard work in connection with the Museum, and the fact that the Museum would bear favourable comparison with those of other towns was , largely due to his efforts. The minutes were seconded by Councillor ELLtsoN and carried. THE POLICING OF THE MARKETS. Counctllor ROBERTS referred to the policing of the Markets, and said he understood the police would only be in attendance at night time, lie thought it had been I decided that the police should patrol the markets day I and night. The MAYOR said they found the markets people themselves kept watch in the day time, and the police were not required. They all agreed that the police should keep watch at night. Alderman SMETHURST : The police are always there Alderman HeTCHINSON observed that they paid the Watch Committee £75 a year for the police to patrol the markets, and the Chief Constable had promised to do all he could to protect the markets from sunset to sunrise.

THE WATER WORKS EXTENSION. SATISFACTORY PROGRESS. At a meeting of the Water Committee on the 19th December, the Engineer reported that the adit at Delph lane had been driven through between shafts Nos. 1 and 2, and that only a short length between shafts Nos. 3 and 4 remained to be completed, and it was resolved that according to the terms of the contractor the new engine at Delph-lane be tested in accordance with the specification. Alderman EVANS,movin,,,,, the adoption of the minutes which were carried, remarked that the whole scheme was now practically finished except the removing of the debris.

PHYSICAL TRAINING IN SCHOOLS. , Councillor TINMON stated that a sub-committee had been appointed to meet a special sub-committee of the School Manhgement Committee of the Education Com- mitteeto go into the question of physical training in the elementary schools of Warrington. It seemed very likely that the Board of Education would depart from their usual custom of advocating instruction being given to children by apparatus. The Swedish drill, which had been agreed upon by a committee composed of military men, schooltnasters, and others, did sway to ' a considerable extert with apparatus. The committees who were going into the matter would be in a position to place before the Council what they thought the best and most satisfactory way of giving physical training to the children. A gymnasium was a vcry costly addi- tion to any school, and while he would be glad to see , one attached to every school, he thought on the score , of expense they could give efficient training without - them if it was properly controlled from the central Gymnasium. OTHER BUSINESS' The minutes of several committees, which we pub- lished last week, were confirmed without comment. The more important debates which were raised will be found reported elsewhere in this paper.

The Influenza Epidemic is again spreading over the country. The visitation is of a very prostrating nature, and the disease is certainly on Vie increase. The origin of the disease or rather the circumstances which bring it about is as much a mystery as ever. You cannot trifle with Influenza. It undermines the Nervous System to a greater extent than almost any other disease and it is Pneumonia that kills, and Pneumonia strikes the man who imagines he has only a mild form of Influenza, and can go about the street just as usual. It can hardly be doubted that the prevalence of Chest and. Lung Complaints must be ascribed to the baneful influence of Influenza, whose after effeets so frequently take this form. It was a bad day for this Country when this malignant scourge first visited our shores. Year after year it has never since failed to revive at its due time, and to extort its tribute of human life. Having observed its ravages, and its dreadful effects upon the constitution on the occasion of its former visits, we cannot but regard its recurrence with much fear, as a great many have not yet, if they ever will, regain their former health. As a Preventative, we know /of nothing equal to Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters. A few strong doses taken in time have often proved effectual in warding off attacks of this Malady, and always succeed in mitigating their severity when a patient is under their influence. If you suffer from any of these symptoms, know that they are the forerunners of Influenza, and it behoves you to resort at once to a course of Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters. By common corsent Patients every- where admit that they feel more depressed and miserable after an attack of Influenza than while under its influence, and more than they feel after an attack of any other illness. dismissed in this fashion. What the dem_ "A lady is present," replied Toni, quietly. Butl I beat a hasty retreat, and left them to themselves. An unhappy state of things followed. Mrs. Milnes was as angry as she dared to be with her fiery. tempered husband; Archie lounged about in gloomy despondency; Ada made herself ill with crying over "papa's cruelty to Theo," and coaxed her mother to put off going to Scotland, declaring she felt too ill to stand the long journey. I had an uneasy suspicion that she managed to correspond with Theo, and to contrive occasional interviews with him. She seemed in better spirits, but was restless, rind so capricious that Masham emphatically re- marked: "There was no bearing with Miss Ada's whims."

"Miss Field, are you too tired to sing ? " Tom asked me one evening, "or is it cruel to beg for the exertion in this atmo sphere ? " It was an intensely hot night at the end of August, and I certainly did not feel inclined to comply with his request ; but music was his greatest pleasure, and almost imperceptibly I had made tfie discovery that to Tom's likes and dislikes I was by no means indifferent. Presently I began his favourite, "Oh, that we two were maying ! ' "Oh, that we two lay sleepily,' under the church- yard sod ! " I was singing, when Ada suddenly broke into passionate weeping.

..;

"-via, (tear, wnat is it r i criea, go,ng to her, while Tom, with a man's dislike to a scene, became absorbed in contemplating the storm-clouds gathering fast and thick around. "I wish you would not sing such horrid, dismal songs," sobbed Ada. "It makes me miserable to hear about being buried, just when I—" She stopped abruptly. "It is the storm in the air that has upset me," she went on, with an odd little laugh, very unlike her usual girlish merriment. "Good-night, Tom. I know you think crying women are a nuisance, so I will go off to bed. Please, Cora, don't come in to me to-night. I am very tired, and would rather be alone." As she spoke a terrific peal of thunder shook the whole house. It was a fearful storm; and even when the thunder ceased, the rain kept on in torrents.

II could not sleep, so wrapping myself in a dlessing-gown I took a book, and getting deeply interested in it, read on and on, heedless of time. The candle was placed on a table in a corner behind me, so that from outside the room must have appeared to be in darkness. Presently a low whistle attracted my attention. The sound seemed to come from immediately below my window. Then a man's voice said:

"Wait ten minutes before you venture. Tom has only just put out the lights in the library. Did you manage those fastenings as I told you, dearest ? " 4 felt puzzled. A thief would not speak of Tom by his Christian name • and as none of the servants slept on this side of the house, to whom could the "dearest" be addressed? I could not hear "dearest's " reply, but imagined it must have been a caution about my quick ears (it was a standing joke in the family that nothing ever escaped them) for there was an emphatic: "Sleeps in that room ? The deuce, she does I" from the man below. Surely, in spite of the cautiously lowered tones, the voice was Theo's. The truth flashed upon me. It was Ada he had come to steal away. My first impulse was to implore her not to trust herself to Theo ' • but I knew her infatuation for him, and if she refused to listen, what could I do? "Tom!" I said to myself. "If the lights are only just put out, he can't be asleep yet." Cautiously opening the door, I sped noiselessly down the corridor, till I came to his room at the far end. He was humming "Oh, that we two were mayino• !;" so vigorously I had to knock four times before he called out a cheery: "Who's there ? " "Mr. Milnes," I cried, desperately, for the precious moments were passing all too quickly. "Why the deuce don't you come in, whoever you are ? " was the response to another application of my knuckles. I heard him stride across the room, and in an instant the door was flung open. "Miss 1 ield !" he exclaimed, in astonishment. I s' •ed to him to be quiet, and in a few rapid word explained my errand. " You must come with me and tate her back, while I tackle that rascal!" he said. "There's not I a minute to lose," and he hurried me downstairs. "Do you know which door Is " he whispered. "No, I did not hear." "Then we must watch."

He pulled me behind the heavy curtains which hung over the drawing-ropm door, apd there we waited. _ _

"Are you frightened, Cora?" he asked, softly. "You are trembling so. I will take care of you. Surely you guess by this time how infinitely precious you are to me—" "Hush!" I said.

"Here she is."

He was holding back one curtain a little, and through the narrow opening I saw Ada coming slowly down the stairs. Her face was very pale, her eyes had a startled, hunted expression. She was dressed for walking, and carried a good-sized 1 bag. We waited till she had passed us, then followed her as she went swiftly towards the morning-room, the French windows of which opened immediately beneath my window. The rain had ceased. The air felt fresh and cool, as Ada pulled open the glass doors and peered anxiously out. "Ada," called Tom, catching her arm. "Toni! You here " she exclaimed, nervously. "I was so restless, I could not sleep; so I—l thought the garden would be cool and pleasant." "What about this heavy bag? Ada, you know you were going to meet that man. Go back to your room, and leave me to speak to him." "Darling, come quick," cried Theo's voice. Some instinct made me spring forward and drag Tom aside. There was a sharp crack; I felt a sudden pain in my shoulder, then all was dark. When I opened my eyes, I was lying on my own bed, with Masham bending over me, and Ada crying by my side. "She will do now, Miss Ada. It was only a bad faint. I hope the doctor will come soon though, for I don't like to meddle with the shoulder. Ah, to think that but for tier, Mr. Tom might be lying dead."

For over a fortnight I was a prisoner in my room; but in spite of the pain and weakness, I was happy, for was I not sure of Tom's love? Ada had gone away on a visit as soon as she heard the doctor's verdict that there was nothing seriously wrong with me. "Don't think me unkind, Cora," she pleaded. "But though I can't help seeing now what Theo is, I did truly love him. I can't bear to stay on here and be talked about. Do you know, they have found the housemaid who left soon after the robbery, and she has confessed that Theo took the rubies, and made her conceal the case in your room, so that suspicion might fall on you. Oh, I can't bear to think how wicked he was!" "What has become of him, dear, do you know?" "They think he has gone abroad. If they searched for him and he was arrested, it would all come out about his persuading me to elope, and—we want all that to be forgotten, if possible. I can never be grateful enough to you, Cora, for saving me from him." Soon after Ada left I had a letter from Mr. Black, saying that a certain American company, in which the greater part of, my small capital was invested, had turned out a most profitable affair. "There is no need, dear child," he wrote, "for you to continue with the Milneses." But I had no wish to leave Fernside. I had not yet seen Toni, having been forbidden by the doctor to go downstairs; but the evening I received this letter, Mr. and Mrs. Manes were going out to dinner, so I thought the house being quiet, I would make my first venture. Masham established me on the morning-room sofa, promising to come in from time to time. Presently the opening of a door made me start. It was Tom. • "What a shame to have disturbed you," he said, taking my hand. "Cora; I have been longing to see you, but they would not let me, so I have had to conteat myself with sending books and flowers. I did not write, because I knew, your right arm being disabled for my sake, you could not answer me. You must have guessed I love you, dear. Will you let me take care of you for evermore? Will you be my wife ? " "But your parents will hate me. I—Ada's com- panion—to marry their eldest son." He reasoned away all My fears, and by the time Masham re-appeared, we had decided that the explanation with Mr. and Mrs. Milnes should take place on the morrow, our wedding soon after Christmas. As I feared, the explanation was stormy, until Tom (who had talked himself hoarse in expatiating on my pedigree) happened to mention that I had shares in the American company Mr. Black had written about. Then Mr. Milnes relented, and was heard to mutter that he wished the shares were his. Ada came to us on a long visit soon after our marriage. Gradually the sadness fell from her, and she was once more her old merry self but I did not feel satisilpri

"Fate seems to have destined you to be the pre- siding genius of my marriage, Cora," she said, when she told me the news. "Through you I was saved from the mad folly of running away with Theo Styles; through you I have learnt to know the man with whom I feel I shall be as happy as you and Tom most certainly are." "Tom is that rare specimen of humanity—an unselfish man," I replied. "You might search the world over, and not find so good, so--" "Oh, spare me!" she interrupted, laughing. "I have just been listening to a long tirade from Tom himself on your perfections. I fully endorse his sentiments, only hearing of so much goodness has a bad effect on me, and makes me feel I must go and do something dreadfully naughty by way of a change. Seriously though, Cora, Ido agree with Tom in looking upon the day that you first came to Fernside as a red-letter one for us all." "Then you think the same as Gerald Massey," I said, holding up the book I had been reading when she came in.

" 'And there be none of all the poorest poor: But they may bring a little human love To mend the world.' I was poor enough when you first knew me, Ada." "That flay be. But I heartily echo the verdict I heard Bertie pronounce the other day, when someone asked him how he liked his new sister. His emphatic reply was: "She is worth her weight in gold I"

SCIENCE NOTES AND GLEANINGS.

ZIMALIIIM is a new alloy of aluminium/ snag- tl es ium, and zinc. It is harder and more easily worked than aluminium.

A SCOTCH scientist attempts to prove from geological phenomena that gravitation is electrical, and that it is identical with terrestrial and solar magnetism.

===== 0002601_19050107_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
NUTRITIVE VALUE OF STERILISED MILK.

According to M. Variot, cow milk sterilised at a temperature of 108 deg. C. preserves all its nutritive value. No case of scorbutic babies has been observed by him when nourished with this milk. Of 3,000 nurslings of the poor class only 3 or 4 per cent, were unable to utilise the milk. Atrophied children have been reared upon it.

===== 0002601_19050107_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FLOWING OF METALS.

It is perhaps not generally known that one of the most important properties of metals employed in striking coins and medals, and stamping and shaping articles of jewellery, is that of flowing iinder pressure. Standard silver is remarkable for this property, which precisely resembles the flowing of a viscous fluid. The flow takes place when the metal is subjected to rolling, stamping, or ham- mering, and the particles of the metal are thus carried into the sunken parts of the die without fracturing, and a perfect impression is produced.

===== 0002601_19050107_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
AN AIR-CURRENT RECORDER.

As it is important for aerial journeys to know the direction and force of the wind at various alti- tudes, M. Julliot, the engineer of M. Lebaudy, the well-known French balloonist, has invented an ap- paratus for the purpose, which is really an anemo- metric balloon. It consists of a small balloon, captive by steel wire, and illuminated inside by an electric lamp for night work. The balloon is allowed to ascend to different altitudes, and the inclination of the wire, together with its pull, are caused to register the force and direction of the wind upon graduated circles, vertical and hori- zontal.- An altitude recorder and an "oriflamme" on the balloon also shew the height and the regu- larity of the air currents.

===== 0002601_19050107_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
EUROPEAN RACE-TYPES.

According to Dr. Deniker, the eminent French anthropologist, Europe contains the "tallest race known," namely, the Highlanders of Scotland. Tall statures are common in the north-west, and medium or short in the rest of Europe. The Swedes are the blondest, and the South Italians the darkest people. Northern Europe is mainly blonde, Southern Europe dark, and Central Europe intermediate. Deniker classifies the population of Europe into six main races or types: (1) A tall, blonde, long-headed, straight-nosed northern race (the "Cymric" race of Broca, and "Teutonic" race of Ripley), with a less tall, blonde, medium-headed, "pug "-nosed sub-race found near it. (2) A blonde, short, square-faced "Eastern" race of Eastern Europe, with a sub-race like it, and very short, occurring in Poland and Prussia, and called "Vistulian." (3) Short, long- headed brunettes, or Mediterranean race. (4) Very short, round-headed, broad-nosed, dark Cavenole or Alpine race. (5) A very dark, fairly tall, long- headed Atlanto - Mediterranean or Littoral race found on the coast from Rome to Gibraltar. (6) A tall, dark, short-headed Adriatic or Dinaric race.

===== 0002601_19050107_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
POWER OF OCEAN WAVES.

At the International Engineering Congress, held att. Louis, some unusual figures were given on the subject of the height and power of ocean waves, particularly as regards their effect upon harbour works. In the course of a paper dealing with the new Dover harbour, it was stated that since these works have been in progress no nave of a greater height than 15ft. has been recorded—a fact which will be very surprising to those who have experienced the miseries of the Dover-Calais passage. The fact is the moi e remarkable because, at the entrance to the Tyne, waves from 35ft. to 40ft. high have been measured, and the last-named height has also been observed at Peterhead. In dwelling upon the necessity for what are known as spending-beaches and wave-traps, for dissipating and controlling wave action, it was stated that the depth to which the latter extends is now known to be much greater than was once commonly supposed. Proof of this is shewn by the fact that lobster-pots, placed in from 120 ft. to 180 ft. of water, have been found to be filled with sand and shingle after a heavy gale; moreover, sand had been found deposited after a heavy gale in the gallery of the Bishop Rock lighthouse, the latter being 120 ft. above the water, and the depth of the water at that point 150 ft. That the water, even at considerable depths, must be moving during a gale with great momentum, is shewn b3r the fact that at the Peterhead breakwater blocks weighing 41 tons, and located over 36ft. below spring tide low-water, I were displaced during a storm, while a section of the breakwater weighing 3,300 tons was moved bodily for a few inches without the brickwork being dislocated.

===== 0002601_19050107_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
SOLAR ELECTRICITY.

A great scientific truth has been demonstrated in regard to the sun's rays. Through the aid of a great sun motor, the Rev. Professor Himalaya, a Portuguese scientist, claims to have proved beyond the possibility of error that the actual origin of the rays and the heat of the sun is electrical. The pyrheliophoro, or sun motor, is the invention of Professor Himalaya. Since it has been in operation the inventor has been making some remarkable experiments with it. He has found that the heat of the sun is more than double that of the electric arc which has hitherto been the highest known tem- perature-6,300deg. Fahrenheit. He has found that there is no known substance, not even asbestos, that will not fuse in his sun furnace, into which the concentrated rays of his mirror reflector and concentrator are thrown. Asbestos always chars, and sometimes fuses. Manganese, the hardest known. metal to fuse, melts and runs as water in the. pyrheliophoro. Iron has no opportunity to melt, so intense is the heat. Instead of melting and running in liquid form, as it would in a blast I furnace, iron shrivels and dries up, and becomes like paper that has been burnt in a fire. Another great discovery that the inventor has made is that no matter how high the temperature to which a substance may be subjected, its mole- cular properties rejnain always the same. Iron, melted or burnt to ash, is still iron; gold, though burnt to a crisp in the pyrheliophoro, is still gold ; manganese does not become something else after it is fused; aluminium is always aluminium, though it is subjected to 12,000kleg. of heat. ThAs the pyrheliophoro successfully explodes the dreams of the alchemists of old who thought to change the baser metals into gold. It cannot be done.

===== 0002601_19050107_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
ARE THE PLANETS INHABITED?

It is not many years, writes M. Camille Plain- marion, the famous French scientist, in Harper's: Magazine, since Mars entered into the sphere of our observation. And one can also say that there is but a small number of the inhabitants of this world who have observed it in all its details, and of these, the most experienced is Signor Sehiaparelli, director of the observatory at Milan. The geographical map of the planet Mars has been made with infinite care by the above-mentioned astro- nomer. One might really consider it a terrestrial sphere of continents, islands, coasts, peninsulas, gulfs, waters. Moreover, clouds, rains, inunda- tions, snows, seasons, winters and summers, springs and autumns, prevail as they do here; and the intensity of the season is absolutely the same as with us, the inclination of the axis being the same as ours. If it were ever attempted to put into practice any project for communication between this world and ours, the signals would have to be established on a very vast scale. It would not be a question of triangles, squares, and circles of some kilometres in size, but of figures more than 100 kilometres large. Moreover, it would always be on the hypotheses that there are inhabitants of Mars, that these inhabitants under- stand astronomy, that they have optical instruments analogous to ours, and that they observe our planet with interest. This planet must be to them a star of first magnitude the evvping and morning star, the brightest star, of their 'heavens. When on a beautiful starry night we examine Mars under a telescope, when we see the polar snows, which melt at spring-time, those finely efined con- tinents, those long-gulfed Mediterraneans, this eloquent and varied geographical configuration, one cannot but ask oneself if the sun which , illumines a world so like our own does not like- wise shed light on living beings, if the rains do I not fertilise crops, if this atmosphere be not inhaled by beings; or if this sphere of Mars, which turns with rapidity in space, be like an empty train on a railway—ivutaining neither passengers nor goods.

AN IMPORTANT BLACK COUNTRY

DEVELOPMENT.

A strenuous attempt to resusciate the Black Country in general and the iron trade in particular, by the intro- duction of Mond gas is about to take practical effect. The first section of the Mond plant has been completed,, and gas is now actually in course of manufacture. Sir Alfred Hickman, one of the largest ironmasters in the district, in conversation with our Birmingham repre- sentative on Thursday said that not only would iron- masters themselves use it extensively, but he knew of most important new industries that were only awaiting the supply ,of gas to establish themselves in the district. As to the economy of the gas he had no doubt what- ever. At the present time, he has engines of 3500 horse power driven by gas and another 1800 horsepower will shortly be added. The success achieved elsewhere particularly in Lancashire, Cheshire and the North of England demonstrated its possibility. The work of preparing the plant at Dudley Port, and laying gas-mains has occupied three years. The Mond Company has a capital of million sterling, and the system when complete is to be the greatest gas produc- ng and distributing plant in the world, it will serve an area covering one hundred and twenty square miles from Walsall to Stourbridge, and taking in the whole of the Black Country towns. The maximum price to be ' charged is 4d. per thciaslnd feet, but the company hope to be able to sell it at 2d. This will compare with Is. 10d. to 2s. per thousand charged by the Birmingham Corporation, for its gas though it should be added that the Birmingham product has four times the calorific value of Mond gas. The extraordinary cheapness of the latter article is due to its production from slack, of which enormous quantitities can be had at a very cheap rate. •

Eyre, superintendent minister, pre- sided; and the Mayor, in declaring the chapel open, said he had a very high regard for the work the Primitive Methodists- were doing, aed for their minister He had sometimes wondered why they bad not spread II their wings more in St. Helens than they had done. ===== 0002601_19050107_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
HOW WE CHANGE OUR SKIN. Interesting Scientific Discovery.

It is strange to think of the human body wearing out just the same as a suit of clothes. Yet a man will " get through" eight or nine different bodies in a lifetime. In sevem years the whole of his bones, muscles, organs, and tissues have been by slow degrees entirely replaced. Some parts of the body wear out sooner than others; notably the skin, because of its exposure and its great liability to injury and disease. New tissue is constantly being produced ; and this, as it forms, is pushed upwards, and it takes the place of the old tissue which becomes horny and is finally cast off.

The fine white dust that one may scrape off the skin consists of these horny scales. An eminent English chemist has devoted years of untiring experiment to this problem of the growth of new skin with a view to dealing with those emergencies when the skin becomes injured, inflamed or diseased. It is believed that this shrewd observer of Nature's ways has found in Zam- buk the ideal balm to use on such occasions, and a balm which, by reason of it being Nature's own healer, will quickly supplant the coarse ointments, salves and em- brocations that have had to suffice in the past. Ever since man acquired the instinct to rub a place that hurt, attempts have been made to discover the right substance for him to rub with. The great chemist already referred to considered that the likeliest place to discover this substance was in the 1 forests of Nature situate beyond the borders of civiliza- tion.

At length, after a wearying search, certain rare plants were found from which could be extracted juices possessing the astonishing power of creating new healthy skin ! When applied to a cut or sore, inflamma- tion and disease would be at once taken out and new ' sound tissue grown completely over the affected plac To this unique extract were added other juices and balsams of high medicinal merit ; the whole was refined and concentrated so as to increase the potency of each element, and Zam-buk, as we now know the great healer, thus came into existence. The secret of its manufac- ture now belongs to the Zam-Buk Company, 4, Red Cross-street. London, E.C.. who have been able to bring the price of the balm within the reach Of all. Zam-buk may be had of all chemists or post free direct from the proprietors, for one and three halfpence a box, or two and nine for a large family size, containing nearly four times the quantity. THE NORTHERN UNION. --- NORTHERN LEAGUE. First Division. -- RESULTS OF SATURDAY'S MATCHES.

G. T. P. *Wakefield T. *Oldham Leigh 1 1 5 *Warrington ... 1 0 2 *Batley 3 1 9 Leeds 2 1 7 *Salford. Hull Kingston R. 2 3 13 Runcorn 2 1 7 Wigan *Widnes.. Played on the ground of the clubs marked *. WARRINGTON v. LEIGH. This important match in both the Northern and South West Lancashire Leagues was played at Warrington and resulted in the first defeat for Warrington at Wilders- pool. The homesters were sadly hamucapped by the absence of all the regular threequarters, while Leigh were at full strength. There were about 5,000 spectators and though on the losing side Warrington had most of the game. Teams:— Warrington.—Hallam, back; Short, Hockenhall, Brooks, and Kenyon, three-quarter backs; Di Davies and Duckworth, half backs; Shugars, Preston, Board- man, Cook, Belton,Phomas, Harmer, and Swift, forwards. Leigh.—Pearson, back; Johnson, Messer, Gartland, and Neville, three-quarter backs; Dunbavin and Burgess, half backs; Kelley, O'Neil, W. Roberts, Berry, Silcock, Webster, Blackburn, and Lee, forwards. Referee, Mr. C. Gibson, Salford. Touch judges, Messrs. E. Gordon, Widnes, and F. Little, St. Helens. Leigh were the first to attack and resolute forward play was met by Hallam, who by degrees caused the visitors to fall back. Twice Preston came near placing penalty goals, but the attack was once more warded off, and Dunbavin and Burgess qpening out the game, War.- rington had another hot spell of defending. Johnson was floored at the corner, but from a scrummage near the line Burgess made no mistake with a pass from Dunbavin, who added the goal from the try. Warring- ton responded well, and after Short had been pulled up at the right corner Kenyon made a big bid at the opposite side. Preston placed a penalty goal, and Leigh led at the interval by 5 points to 2. In the second half Leigh led off with a desperate forward rush, which Hallam checked in superb fashion, and a series of free kicks placed Warrington in an advantageous position. Preston had several shots at goal, but all went astray, and though Warrington attacked the major portion of this half there was no flaw in Leigh's defence. Result :—Leigh, 1 goal and 1 try (5 points), Warrington, 1 goal (2 points).

LANCASHIRE COMBINATION.

WARRINGTON V. LEIGH._

Played at Leigh, on Saturday, before a capital attendance. Warrington were short of a man. COMPLICATIONS FROM A COLD. How Bile Beans Cure Liver Chill.

Many are the complications which may arise from a mere cold. Bile Beans, by toning up the liver and diges- tive system, strengthen the weak places made by cold and chill.

As an illustration of their power in this directisn, the case of Mr. Thomas Brady, of Mansfield-street; Liver- pool, might be mentioned. Mr. Brady served for two years in South Africa during the Boer war. He with- stood the terrible trials of the hot sun by day and the keen frost by night, which laid so many of his comrades low. Returning to the coast, however, from Johannes- burg he caught cold, and complications resulted. " I had pains in the small of my back," he says, "and dull, heavy pains in my side and in the pit of the stomach."

What Mr. Brady reajly sufferecilfrom was a form of liver chill, very common in Englandthroughout winter. " My body got !rite bent," he says, "because of my inability to straighten myself when the pain was so bad I to hobble about like an old man doubled up with age. and indeed I had the greatest difficulty in getting about at all. My appetite failed me, and I became very weak and depressed. When I got home I had some medicine and applied plasters to where the pain was most acute. Still I kept on sintering, nothing seeming to bring me the slightest relief. "A friend told me I ought to try Bile Beans. I did so, and in!a short time they rid me not only of the pain' but of the weakness, loss of appetite, and debility which had resulted. lam now in excellent health ; indeed I have just this moment returned from work, where I have been hard at it for three days and two nights without any rest or sleep. That kind of thing cannot be done unless one is in excellent health, which, I am glad to say, lam now. This I owe to Bile Beans." MOTOR NOTES. •

We are pleased to note that a driver, who was recently convicted of driving recklessly, has been sent to prison for two months withont the option of a fine.

The case was a particularly bad one. The chauffeur had taken out his masters's car, without permission,and at two o'clock in the morning—according to one account —leapt an excavation at Westminster Bridge, Oft. long, 4ft. deep and 4ft. Gins, in width. In court he explained that he did not pull up sooner because it would have been dangerous to use his breaks with the road in so greasy a condition. The magistrates, however, con- sidering that the excuse was feeble and that it was to be assumed the same argument would be supposed to apply whether the obstacle before the car hapdened to be a man, woman, or child, sentenced the driver as stated, and it is to be hoped that during the next two months, the young man will ruminate upon the advisa- bility of driving carefully over a "slippy "surface so as to avoid the necessity for choosing between immediate destruction and jamming the brakes dead on and so in- viting disaster in another form.

It is greatly to be regretted that so many paid drivers should be so utterly reckless in their methods. The fact of the matter is that many of them are recruited from a class to whom mechanical principles do not appeal in the least, and they have really no conception whatever of the damage they inflict on engine and tyres alike by perpetually driving at the highest rate of speed the motor can achieve. Keep her going at a little under her full power and nurse her when necessary, and the fife of the engine will be appreciably prolonged, as, too, will be the life of those expensive and important accessories, the tyres. Yet how many of the ordinary paid drivers ever do nurse their cars? How many of them know how? How many. indeed, drive any other way than with the timing fully advanced, the throttle wide open, and the right hand ready to push on the emergency brake, in conjunction with the quick application of that actuated by the foot directly any occasion arises for the sudden diminution o f the speed.

Nobody seems quite to know what has become of the Anerley Show which was to have been held at the Crystal Palace early in January. Some declare that it has been abandoned ; others are as firmly convinced that it will materialise yet. Certainly it can well be dis- pensed with, for, without that one,there are three other important shows to take place between now and the end of March. The manufacturer is already committed to as much expense as he can stand with these, and, indeed this multiplication of shows is not only tedious but is apt to become extremely wearisome.

the exhibition at Olympia, due to open on February 10th, is the one upon which the hopes of the amateur buyer are mostly based. Mr. Cordingley's show at Islington is always well attended, but it occurs just a little too late in the season, coming as it does at a time when purchasers really want to be driving their cars and not merely to be placing orders for them— orders which, possibly, cannot be executed for some weeks to come. At Olympia, on the other hand, the various vehicles will be shown in such good time as will allow a prospective buyer to place his motor and even obtain delivery of his car before the season opens. This is important, especially when the manufacturers are placing novelties upon the market, for otherwise those who might have been likely to invest have already made their choice for the conking season. And there will be several novelties at Olympia, if report speaks truly, whilst it is also probable that it will be here that a rather startling scheme will be first propounded by the Motor Car Company, of whioh we are not at liberty to write more fully just yet.

Like the cycle shows, the motor car exhibitions have had many homes; but it is rather suprising that amongst them all the Alexandra Palace has never yet been favoured with practical consideration. It is reached by an admirable train service—which is certainly mere than can be said for the Crystal Palace—and possesses some fine grounds wherein trial runs for intending pur- chasers could well be held.

FAILURES IN ENGLAND AND WALES.—According to "Kemp's Mercantile Gazette," the total number of failures in England and Wales during the week ending Friday, December 30th, was 90, viz.:—New bankruptcy proceedings published in the London Gazette, 41; deeds of arrangement registered, 49. The sespective numbers in the corresponding week of last year were: bank- ruptcies, 39 ; deeds of arrangement, 54—total 93 ; being a net increase in bankruptcies of 262, and a net increase in deeds of arrangement in1904,t0 date, of 476.

FOOTBALL TEAM IN' DANGER,

On Monday afternoon, as the Hull football team were being driven from the railway station to the Runcorn headquarters in a waggonette, the driver lost the reins, and in endeavouring to recover them fell between the horses, which started off at a wild gallop. Mr. Charles- worth, the secretary, jumped out of the vehicle, which almost collided with the Market-place lamppost. Two constables pluckily seized the reins and brought the horses Via standstill.

Diagram 45 shows a run-through in-off from the red. Both the object balls are dead on the top cushion, and are too near to each other to allow of a run cannon being made from the position of the cue ball. The run-through in-ow,should be played exactly as though the object, _white were not in the way. If the red be struck cor- rectly it will move the white from where it is, and will itself get away in time to leave a clear path for the cue ball. In playing a stroke of this nature it will often happen that the second object ball will rebound from the side cushion right on to the cue ball, so that tne result of the stroke will be a kiss cannon instead of an in-off. In fact the kiss cannon is often far easier. to get than the run-through. Nevertheless, it is always as well to play for the in-off, using plenty of side (left hand side in the diagram), as in this way there is a double chance of scoring. The run-through in-off would be on with the object balls nearer to each other than shown on the diagram, but when they are very near to each other no in-off will be on owing to the cue ball catching up the first object ball before it has had time to get away after its contact with the other ball. RUN-THROUGHS PLAYED WITH BOTTOM.

Most ordinary players have an idea that all run-throughs should be played with top, but top though it increases the forward rotation of a cue ball can often be advantageously dispensed with when the cue ball is not very near the ob- ject ball. When a cue ball is hit at the centre it develops perfect rotation very soon after being struck, and will follow on after a full contact with an object ball, provided that the object ball previous to the stroke was some little dis- tance away. This may very easily be tested as follows:—Place a ball on the centre spot (or a foot nearer to baulk), and play .full on to it from the centre of the D line, striking the cue ball at its centre. It will be found that the cue ball will follow on quite easily after contact with the object ball. Now place the object ball on the pyramid spot, and play again from the centre of the D line striking the cue ball quite low, and using a medium-pace to fairly fast stroke. It will be found that notwithstanding the low hit- ting of the cue ball it will still follow on after contact with the object ball. The reason that this is so is because although the low hitting of the cue ball imparts reverse rotation to the ball, the friction with the cloth checks and finally stops this reverse rotation long before the object ball is reached, and from the moment that the backward rotation becomes exhausted forward rotation sets in. All long distance run-throughs when not played at a fast pace are played with plenty of bottom or drag by all good players, firstly because when side has to be used it is retained by the cue ball much longer than it would be without the drag, and secondly because drag while it lasts ensures the true running of the

E.C. (Burnley)—You could not do better than ask your fellow townsmen W. Holt, his opinion of crysta- late balls. In a recent match with M. Inman extending over a week, which was played with ti,e.,a balls he made breaks of 110, 164, and 204, and wr s most exciting game by the narrow margin of elev. points. Previous to thisllolt's record break was I_o made with ivory balls. It is a wonderful performance to beat ones record break four times in a week and by such a large margin too, and no better testimony to the value of crystalate balls could possibly be given. As I have often pointed out before the ordinary ivory balls in use in clubs and hotels can seLZom be depended on for scientific billiards, and even carefully selected ivory balls are seldom really true, and the proof of this is that one constantly reads of ivory balls having to be changed in important matches through one of the players finding his ball untrue. With crystalate balls such a thing is unknown.
