Brick-street Church Tea Party, Warrington.

The annual tea party and concert in connection with the above church, were held in the schoolroom on Monday. A large number of persons sat down to the excellent tea which had been provided The concert was presided over by Mr. James Shaw, the Church President. The reports of Sunday school and Church work were read by Messrs, Albert Kay and B. Warburton. Two solos entitled, " Only once more " and " Going to Kildare " were sung by Madame Christian for which she received well merited encores. Miss Bather sang very sweetly. " There ain't another daddy in the world like mine" and" Blue- bell," the audience demanding an encore for the former one. Mr. William Jowett sang" Dreams of Paradise" and" The Kings Own," and Mr. A. May- hall sang " The Diver" and Ben Bolt." Mr. A. Grounds was responsible for the humorous items and created a deal of amusement. LIFE-BOAT SERVICES 1N 1901.

The year 1904,unlike its predecessor, was by no means a stormy one, although during thelearlier months a fair amount of boisterous weather was experienced. This re- turned now and againthroughout the year, afterperhaps many weeks of comparative calm,with thelresult that the aid of Life-Boat crews of the Royal National Life-Boat Institution was required, they thus being afforded the opportunity of rendering many valuable life-saving services. The heaviest gales of 1904 occurred on the 11-13th of February, and the 0-16th Deeember. On the first occasion there were 20 launches of Life-Boats, re- sulting in the saving of 17 lives and 5 vessels ; whilst during the latter gale 48 lives and 3 vessels were saved as a result of 35 launches. Between the Ist January and the 31st December.l9o4, inclusive, the Institution's Life Boats were launched 204 times on service' in addi- tion to which Life-Boat crews were assembled in readi-

ness for service 50 times, but their assistance was not needed. The number of lives saved by Life-Boats and other means for which the Royal National Life-Boat institution granted rewards during the year was 519, and as many as 34 vessels and boats were also saved by the Life-Boats from partial or total loss. The total number of lives for the saving of which the Institution has granted rewards since its establishment in 1824 has been 41,880. The cost of maintaining the Institution's large fleet of 285 Life-Boats in a high state of efficiency grows year by year ; but the financial support received from the public in the shape of annual subscriptions and donations is quite insufficient for the purpose. Increased help is, therefore, earnestly appealed for. Annual sub- scriptions and donations will be thankfully received and acknowledged by the Secretary, Mr. Charles Dibdin, 20 Charing Cross Road, London, W.C., by any of the Branch Honorary Secretaries, and by all th? Bankers in the United Kingdom.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENT. Authors of letters intended for publication must in all cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith. Matters intended for publication must be written on one side °MI of the paper and sent in, if possible, not later than Thursday. -

Mr. William Long and the Non-Trading Agitation. Sir,—lt is lamentable to notice how the opinions of many of the so-called leading men " of Warrington have become warped, and their views of the recent agitation twisted, owing to their Companies that have fattened by trading with the Corporation. Take the case of a well known citizen, Mr. William Long, and note what he said at the annual meeting of the Warring- ton Savings Bank on the 20th alt. Here are the words he spoke in reference to the movement :—" Warrington he hoped, would soon be settled down to her work again after that unfortunate agitation—and he still held a strong opinion upon it. It was a very ill-timed and badly managed agitation and one which had certain- ly not redounded to the credit of Warrington." Now, Sir, what is this opinion worth? It is easy to speak of the moveraent as "a very ill-timed and badly managed agitation," and if an impartial man had said this, some weight might have been attached to his words. But when turn to a recently compiled list of the shareholders of the Warrington Slate Company, Limited, (which, as everybody knows, was the worit sinner in the matter of trading with the Warrington Corporation) I am amazed, and perhaps amused, to find that this very Mr. William Long is or was the second largest shareholder in this Company, and that he is or was one of its Directors. One can well under- stand him saying that the agitation was " ill-timed," and one can only say that the opinion of so interested and prejudiced a witness is absolutely worthless. It is, as I have said, lamentable to find how great a number of Warrington men are interestedone way or another in this trading. In fact, it looks as though " the trail of the serpent is over them nil." lam thankful to think that we hay", by this" ill-timed" movement, once for all, killed the demoralizing trading.—Yours, &c. S. NORBLTRY WILLIAMS.

sth January, 1905.

Stockton Heath Tram Fares. Sir,—Alderman Smethurst at the last Council meeting under cover of my inability to reply, owing to the standing orders, stated I was one of the two most extravagant members of the Council,—this distinction from snch an authority is as ridiculous as untrue. I have long since had the opinion that the Warrington Tramways are about the worst in the kingdom with respect to excessive fares and the through running arrangements. The Manager recommended Id. on Stock- ton Heath route, and it was rejected, but only a few months ago he was asked to report upon half-penny fares, and the badly needed overlapping stages, and we have heard nothing further about (outside the committee.) The workmens cars as promised when Parliamentary powers were sought, have never been properly com- plied with, although early cars are not necessary on all routes; still at night, the Committee have no right to charge the ordinary fare for workmen. The charges on Stockton Heath route will create a worse feeling and will not encourage the people to travel as inaquently to Warrington to do their shopping, neither will the extra id. stop a single resident from removing to Stockton Heath. The chief reason how- ever of, mine, after the question of distance is ignored, is to encourage the poorer people in Warrington, who cannot afford to live outside, to avail themselves of a then poor Id. ride for adults and Id. for children, as compared with other places. The extra halfpenny for 500 yards, especially for children, is a stupid imposition, and as a private 'bus company could make the total distance pay with penny fares, it shews little credit to our Tramways to-day. It is. a course, well understood in Warrington, the height of absurdity to ridicule committee's work, and as baths and bowling greens are regarded by the Chair- man of the Tramways as my speciality, permit me to state that if it were practicable I would advocate same in almost every word, if differently arranged with regard to facilities and charges than those at present obtained. —Yours faithfully PETER PEACOCK. 135, Bewsey-road, Warrington, January Gth, 1901.

The Non-Trading Commissioners' Report. Sir,—l observe that the statement which I know to have originally emanated from the Town Clerk's Mace —that f gave the order for the printing of the Non- Trading Commissioners' Report, has been completely disproved by the evidence of the three Commis- sioners and of the printers. I also notice that in the "Report" of the Deputy Town Clerk to the Town Council he does not nowallege that I stated to him that I had given the "order," although his reference to the subject is put so disingeniously that many unwary readers may so understand it. He says that on my tell- ing him that the Report (that is the manuscript or draft of the Non-trading Commissioners report) was ready, he said that if I would let him have it he would take it to the "Guardian" office, and let me have a proof, and that I then said I have giren it (that is the manuscript or draft report, not the order) to Messrs. Walker & Co. Now it is the fact that I did actually carry the draft report from the Commissioners to the printers, but on the other hand, the reason alleged for my so doing is simply ridiculous in view of the fact that I did not give the order at all. The order was given personally by the Commissioners to Messrs. Walker and Co., on the 19th October with full instructions as to size, quality of paper, type, &c.; when it was given the Commissioners had no idea that the Corporation would pay; the point in fact was not I thought of by them, nor indeed by use until the Deputy Town Clerk introduced it to me in our conversation the following day. The sole and the whole concern of the Commissioners (and of myself) was to get the Report completed, and to hand a printed copy (as most suitable satisfactory and expeditious) to the Mayor and to the Burgesses at the earliest possible moment. The manner in which my innocent reference to the accidental meet- ing of Mr. Walker and the Commissioners from which the order ultimately resulted), has been deliberately divorced from its only connection and made into a reason for my giving an order which I never did give, is only one more sad proof of the condition into which the public life in Warrington has been degraded. My career in Warrington is happily known to all now, and I defy any man to show that either in my business or public life I have ever sought to give ordivert an order for directly or indirectly interested motives. And so far,' as regards this paltry printing order, had it been mine to I give, which it was not, it would not have gone where it did for very obvious reasons. Whatever the Deputy Town Clerk may now report, after the lapse of 21. months, he knows perfectly well, as I know, that he himself introduced to me on the 20th October the question of the liability of the Corporation for the printing of the Report ; he knows moreover, that it was not a case of his agreeing, or conceding, but of his insistence that the Corporation should con- tract and pay for the initial printing and for a reasonable number of copies for the Non-trading Asso- ciation. He certainly did at first demur to the foolscap size but most clearlf and distinctly he took over the liability for the cost of printing, and of a number of copies (afterwards settled at 50 copies) for the Non- Traders. In view of the actual position and of the facts, and of the mean discreditable action of the Town Council on Tuesday last, it would almost seem that the whole thing has been a "plant, i on the part of certain officials and members of the Council to tempt the Non- Traders into a false position ; if so, I think the rate- payers will see through the ruse. The plain fact is that these men, many of them,remember the days of reckless waste and ceaseless extravagance when things were made pleasant all round ;. they pine to have these days back again, hence their random and venomous hatred of the men who have spoilt the game! Is it to be wondered at that men of standing and position will not stoop to enter th e Council? I have never said to the Deputy-Town Clerk nor to anyone else that I had had a great deal to do with the report ; the contrary indeed is the fact, and I was sorry that I could not afford any assistance whatever in the arduous duties of the Commissioners until the last night when I merely copied several sheets of the draft report and certain evidence from tha inquiry minutes. I have now done with the Deputy-Town Clerk, and I shall take care to have no further business connection over the telephone with the Town Clerk's °face. "Once bit twice shy,"l and as to the relative reliability of the Town Clerk or the Deputy Town Clerk and myself. I am more than content to leave my reputation in the hands of those who know me. What others may believe is of very little moraeat to me. At the same time I do not believethere is a single burgess of Warrington outside the trading clique who would for a moment harbour a suggestion that I would seek to transfer any liability of mine to the ratepayers or to anyone else. I don't think the Council discussion claims muck notice. Nobody now takes Councillors Maginn,Wilkin- son and Tinnion seriously, but I must however, once for all express my amazement at the temerity of Alderman Bennett in intervening in these trading debates. "0 wad some power the giftie gie us To see oursels as others see us." Here is a man who has had +l5O of the ratepayers' moneys, as I believe improperly and illegally, who in his evidence before the Commissioners admitted that he had doubts as to the legality of his taking such money, and whose conscience so troubled him that he more than once consulted the Town Clerk on the sub- ject, this, I say Is the man whohas theaudacity to stand up and lecture the Non-Traders on having brought dis- credit upon Warrington, when he knows full well that he and his trading friends are the men who had brought discredit on the town. When he has followed the ad- vice I gave him the last time I saw him, and has dis- gorged moneys illegally received as I contend, when in short he has pulled the beam out of his own eye.it will be time enough for him to preach to others. In the

Eli

meantime let him hide his head and hold his tongue.— Yours sc.,

JAMES DODDS. 6th January, 1903. P.S.—As a further proof of the unreliability of the Depnty Town Clerk's report. I need only refer to the last paragraph thereof, wherein he positively asserts that the whole cost of thelwhole 300 copies of the Non- Trading Commissioner's report was chargeol to the Cor- poration. The fact is that the Non-Trading Association holds the receipt for an account dated December 2nd last for the printing of 150 of the 200 copies they received.

Sir,—Allow me to correct a statement I made in my speech at the Council meeting on Tuesday. I said that Mr. Dodds had handed to the Infirmary all he had received in connection with his membership of the Council and of the County Rate Joint Committee, excepting his actual out-of-pocket expenses. I ought to have said that Mr. Dodds did not even deduct his out-of-pocket expenses. I ought also to have said that of the large amounts Mr. Dodds paid for printing in connection with the bye-elections in Bewsey and Town Hall Wards in October and November last not one penny was paid to Messrs. John Walker and Co. In view of these facts, the miserable attempts of some of the Town Hall officials, and of some members of the Council to connact Mr. Dodds with an attempt to give or divert an order from interested motives is as mean as it is contemptible.—Yours JOHN PENIBERTON. January sth, 1905.

Messrs. John Walker and Co, and Mr. Wilson's Statement.

The following letter has been addressed by us to the editors of the Guardian" and " Observer." Sir,—There are two statements in the report on the above, presented at Tuesday's Council meeting by Mr. Stanley Wilson, which we desire to correct as they have special reference to the disputed printing account. Mr. Wilson did not order 150 copies of the report "at a fair price to be afterwards agreed upon." Such a phrase, or anything equivalent to it, was never used in the course of the interview between Mr. Wilson and our Mr. Walker. Mr. Wilson gave us to understand that the Corporation would not pay any " fancy " price, but he ordered the copies without any farther reference to terms. Mr. Wilson also says in his report : " I have since ascertained that the £23 10s. charged to the Corpora- tion represents the cost of printing the whole 300 copies of the report, of which the Corporation only received 100." This is entirely a misstatement of fact, presum- ably due to some misunderstanding. The charge to the Corporation (of £22, and not £23 10s.) represents the whole cost of typesetting and printing the first 150 copies, and was made in accordance with the arrange- ment which Mr. Wilson verbally agreed to, i.e. that this initial cost should fall upon the Corporation. The charge for the second 150 copies was sent to the Non- Trading Commissioners on December 2nd last, and has since been paid. Yours faithfully, JOHN WALKER & CO. Jan. sth, 1905.

WARRINGTON EISTEDDFOD.

SUCCESSFUL NEW YEAR'S GATHERINGS.

Victory of Crosfield's Band.

=

_„.,,..,-,(",........./.

, It was a happy augury to the new year that the people of Warrington should show their appreciation of local efforts to encourage musical development by making the third annual Eisteddfod, which was held in the Parr Hall on Monday, a greater success than either of the preceding festivals. The promoters have reason to feel pleased with the results of their enterprise, in as much as the transplanting of an essentially Welsh institution to Warrington was a somewhat hazardous undertaking, and required not a little optimism as well as sound management It speaks volumes for the perseverance and capability of those entrusted with the work, that the public are beginning to realise more fully the usefulness of these annual competi- tions which cannot fail to have an influence for good. The choice of date for the third festival was a particu- larly fortunate one. As most people were keeping 1 holiday, and the weather was not favourable to out- 1 door enjoyment, the eisteddfod was a welcome attrac- tion. At the afternoon meeting the attendance was , good, while in the evening the hall was filled and great enthnsiasm was displayed. Several new features which ! aroused keen interest were included in the programme 1 chief among them being the local brass band contest 1 This item was arranged as a wind-up to the afternoon proceedings, and although to the average listener it was perhaps tedious to hear five bands go through the same long selection, each body of instrumental- lists boasted an enthusiastic following who applauded the performances with evident pleasure. The victory of Messrs. Crosfield's band was an immensely popular one. Another new departure was that in the juvenile solo competition,eachreentrant was allowed to render a song of his or her own choice, and the variety proved acceptable. The Rev. W. Wynn Davies, of Liver- pool, conducted each meeting with a geniality which delighted the audience and contributed in no small measure to the general good humour. As musical adjudicator, Mr. Herbert Whittaker, conductor of the famous Blackpool choir which was successful at the National Eisteddfod, discharged his duties—at times rendered exceptionally difficult by very keen competition—in a careful and tactful way. His decisions appeared to give general satisfaction. Mr. James Brier, of Bradford, acted as band adjudicator and the art adjudicators were :—Messrs. Turner and Co., Rylands-street, Mr. J. M. Greenhalgh, Bridge- street, and Mr. H. Bond, Wilderspool Causeway. FRETWORK.

The prize of ss. for the best fretwork photo frame was awarded to Mr. Thomas Timperley, Warrington.

LOCAL BAND CONTEST.

The afternoon programme concluded with the loos brass band contest for which the test piece was " Stradella " (Flotow). For the first prize Li was offered, and there was a second prize of £2. The fol- lowing bands competed: Crosfield's Brass Band (Mr. W. Halliwell, conductor) ; League of the Cross (Mr. T. Hynes) ; Borough, (Mr. R. Rimmer) ; Legh street, (Mr. F. Podmore),Penketh Tannery,(Mr. W. 11. Harper. The adjudicator listened to the performances from the seclusion of the organ chamber. At the conclusion he said that between three bands the playing had been very close. One mistake some had made was that they appeared to think they were playing in the open air. That was a very fine sounding hall and the least over- blowing soon told. The first prize undoubtedly went to Messrs. Crosfield's band and the second to Penketh Tannery. The decision was received with great enthu- siasm. We have since ascertained that the other bands were placed in the following order :-3, Borough ; 4, League of the Cross ; 5, Legh street.

===== 0002601_19050107_art0150.txt | included=true | included =====
SECOND CHORAL COMPETITION.

• The evening meeting opened with a competition for church or chapel choirs, "And the glory from the " Messiah" being the chorus selected. Only two choirs competed :—Bewsey-road Wesleyan (conductor, Mr. B. Wadsworth), and Leh-street Primitive Methodist (Mr. A. Spruce). The adjudicator said that any church or chapel possessing a choir as good as the two which had been heard that night, had reason to be very well pleased. There were good voiies and intelligent singers in each choir, and one was only a shade better than the other. The Bewsey-road choir showed good tone in every part, and each part as it entered took up its lead very firmly. The soprano was particularly bright and one might almost find .fault on the ground that it was inclined to predominate, especially in view of the weakness of the altos at times. The rendering was rather too slow and dignified for tho strength of the choir. The Legh-street choir was con- siderably better in the amount of tone in the alto part, and on the general balance and slightly better temp) these were points which weighed in favour of this choir by one mark. The sopranos were harder in quality and not quite so good as in the other choir. The prize was awarded to Legh-street choir amid considerable enthusiasm.

YOUTHFUL TEM PERA.NCE Ei YISr3.

The Rev. W. WYSS DAVIES saiii he received three children's essays on "Temperance," a prize of ss. bein g offered. The competitors were Elarold Johnson, Thomas Hewitt, and T. Oultra.m. With the exception of a few sentences they could well see the impress of a child's mind on each of the productions. Several rather amusing extracts were quoted, such as the following:-.- "We have no need to drink any bear, spirits, or any kind of wine; if drink were kept out of the home, all the family would perhaps grow up to be Rechabites ; too much intoxication causes heart disease, whioh very quickly kills people ; sometimes intemperate people gather unhealthy fatness, the fat being put down in wrong places, where no fat should be. The prize was obtained by T. Oultmm, Orford View, Longford.

MIXED VOICE QUARTETTE COMPETITION.

The Lyric quartette party, of Warrington, succeeded in winning the prize of 21 for the best interpretation of "Moonlight and music "(Pinsuti). The adjudicator said it was noticeable how evenly balanced the voices were, and the opening of the piece was beautifully sub- ' clued. The marks were given in the following order : Lyric quartette, 55; St. Helen's party, 54 ; Ariso quartette, Widnes, 50; Prince's quartette, Liverpool, 49. It was in the beautful blending of the voices that the Warrington competitors excelled.

CFIALLENGE SOLO COMPETITION.

Exceptional interest centred in this item, the com- petitors being Mr. Fred Edward Ashton, who sang " How vain is man," Miss A. Woodward, of Stockton Heath, who gave " There's a land ," Mr. E. H. Pearce,of Warrington. who selected "Why do the nations" and Miss Ada Roherts, of Buckley, North Wales, whose effort was "With verdure clad." The prize of 153. and a gold centre medal was awarded to Miss Roberts who, said the adjudicator, gave a capital performance. She possessed a fine voice, and the runs were splendidly executed. Mr. Pearce also came in for commendation, but the adjudicator said that, no doubt in his anxiety to give a spirited rendering, the performance was scarcely dignified enough. Miss Woodward sang very tastefully, but once 2,r twice she was inclined to get out of tune. Mr. Edwards had a tine voice and was unfortunate in missing a lead.

BARITONE OR BASS SOLD. The spirited song "King and country" (Jude) was rendered by Mr. Lewis Greenwood, Widnes; Mr • Woodall, St. Helens: and Mr. C. Rigby, St. Helens. The last-named gentleman gained the prize of 155., the adjudicator remarking that he made a great deal more of the song than the other competitors, his expression being particularly good.

CONTIIALTO SOLO. The final competitors were Miss Woodward, of Stock- ton Heath, Miss JOtieS of Liverpool. and Miss H. Griffiths, of at Helens, the song being "Flight of ages" (Bevan). Miss Griatlas was the winner of the 1.55. prize theadjudicator stating that although her rendering was, alittle'sotnbre,her voicejhad more power than:the others and a good tone. THE CHIEF CHORAL CONIPETETION. WARRINGTON'S CHOIR'S CREDITABLE PERFORNIANCE.

The event of the day was the competition for male choirs, which attracted a capital entry. A silver Clip and £l2 12s. were the first prize, and there was a second award of £2. The test piece Martyrs of the Arena (Da Rifle) --.07,14 splendidly given by the following choirs :—Liverpool Brunswick (con- ductor, Mr. H. E. Marriott) ; Crewe Apollo (Mr. J. Stubbs); Prescot (Mr. H. Berrev); Warrington (Mr. W. S. Nesbitt); Wigan Harmonic (Mr. R. A. Moss); South- port Vocal Union (Kr. J.C. Clarke). Southport proved the premier prize winners, the adjudicator commenting upon their striking unison singing which sounded almost I like one voice. The solo quartette was a capital blend. The attack and tone of the choir were specially notice- able points. The Warrington choir showed good tone of rich quality, especially in the opening I The quartette was not a very good blend, but the general movement of the piece was I exceedingly well chosen. After giving detailed com- ments on the renderings by the other choirs, Mr. Whittaker announced that he awarded the marks as follows :—Southport, SG; Warrington, 5 ; Prescot, 54; IWigan, 54: Crewe, 53; Liverpool, 48. The Wanrington choir's achievement naturally created much pleasure, especially in view of the fact that they were only one marklbehind a well-known organization like Southport. Before the close, the Rev. WYNN DAVIES heartily I congratulated the committee of the eisteddfod on the decided success of both the afternoon and evening meetings. That was the third eisteddfod, and he thought all would agree that it had proved far superior to the other two. Another festival would be held next year, I probably on New Year's Day. He congratulated the I committee on having provided a counter holiday attrac- tion which would elevate the moral, artistic and best 'nature of those who were present. (Applause.) Their I thanks were due to the Chairmen, Councillor Brough and Mr. Lloyd Jones, who had been so considerate as to forego their speeches; and to the ladies who had kindly come forward to invest the winners with their medals, &c. In affairs of this kind there was a vast amount of secretarial work, but in Mr. R. W. Cook they had an admirable secretary who had already developed into an exemplary official. Mr. Holt, the treasurer, was a host in himself, and the general officials and the committee had all worked with tact and energy. With regard to the adjudicator he need only say that no eisteddfod had been so well satisfied as they were with ltfr. Whittaker. (Applause).

Death of Mr. E. L. Fletcher.

On Thursday a cablegram was received by Mr. W. J. Forshaw, solicitor, Warrington, announcing the ' death that day at Melbourne, Australia, of Mr. Ernest !Lyon Fletcher, eldest son of the late Mr. Thomas Fletcher (founder of the firm of Messrs. Fletcher, I Russell and Co. Ltd., Warrington, Pendleton, and , London). The deceased gentleman was a director of the firm up to the time of his departure for Australia lin 1900. He was formerly a Captain in the Warring- ton Volunteers and for two years held the office of President of the Warrington Rugby Football Club. He was abaut thirty-three years of age. Whilst living at Weaverham Mr. Fletcher, who was of a generous and genial disposition, was extremely popular and the news of his death will be received with great regret. His last visit to' Warrington was made at Christ nas, 1903.

THE WATER SUPPLY.

OVERSPENDING ON THE RECENT WORKS.

£20,510 to be Borrowed.

COUNCILLOR PEMBERTON'S COMPLAINT.

The requirements of the Warrington Waterworks undertaking were again discussed at the Council meeting on Tuesday, and ultimately the borrowing 0fj£20,510 to continue the works, was sanctioned. Alderman FRANCOMR, chairman of the Water Com- mittee, again introduced the question in the following resolution :—" That the Town Clerk be instructed to apply to the Local Government Board for their sanc- tion to the borrowing of the sum of £20,510 for water works purposes." At the previous meeting of the Council : he moved a similar resolution which however was not carried as the council asked for more informa- tion as to why the money was required. In proposing the resolution on Tuesday Alderman FRANCOALB said: It will be remembe-ed by many members of this Council that by the proposed Water Bill of 1899 it was the intention of the Corporation to purchase land in the Dennow and at Daresburv, in addition to the works at Winwick and Houghton dreen. The scheme, therefore, comprised a much larger exten- sion than the one just completed, and was estimated to cost £282,000. When the Bill was before the House of Commons, owing to the serious opposition offered, it was decided to withdraw the Dennow and Daresbury sections. The estimate was amended accordingly, and sanction obtained for borrowing £147,000 instead of £232,000, as was at first intended. Professor Boyd Dawkins predicted that we should get 1,200,000 gallons of water per day, and that two engines of 90 horse power each would be sufficient to cope with this quantity, and would allow one of the engines to be always resting, so as to be ready in case of a breakdown. But this prediction did not obtain in actual working. Instead of getting 1,200,000 gallons of water per day we got nearly 4,000,000 gallons, and the two small engines had to be abandoned, and we were recommended to purchase the present powerful engines 120 h.p., and pumps, in order to deal with the abundant water supply. It is quite true that we have made all preparations for duplicating the present engine and pumps if it is found necessary to do so, and I think the committee deserve the credit of the ratepayers for looking ahead as they have done. (Hear, hear.) We hope, in the near future to be receiving a large revenue from our customers outside the borough, but who live within our limit of supply, and when that takes place we shall hope to be is aposition to duplicate the engines and pumps at Dolph-lane. On several occa- sions during the last few months the attention of the Water Committee has been informally called to the fact that the £147,000 would not be sufficient to com- plete the whole of the works. On the 7th of November last Mr. Fdirhurst, our borough treasurer, informed the Water Committee that we had overspent the money sanctioned by .C 2,905 Ss. 2d., and requested us to pass a resolution empowering the Corporation to apply to the Local Government Board for power to borrow a further sum of money required to complete the works. At this time the Water Engineer was asked to prepare an esti- mate of the amount of money it would take to complete the works. Well, this has been done, and Mr. Deas gives us the following statement :—Amount overspent on October 31, £2,905 its. 2d.; to complete No. 12 con- tract, sinking wells and retention money, £B,BOO ; duty test of engine and pumps at Houghton Green, £1,060; bitumen lining for reservoir at Winwick, £122 12s. ; cost of laying mains as per agreement, £2,622 55.; to estimated cost of mains within the borough during the next five years, £2,000 : to estimated cost of mains during the next five years outside the borough, £3,000; making £20,510 Bs. 2d. lam pleased to say the work is now finished, and has proved a great success, and we i have an abundant water supply ; n fact, we have more than three times the quantity of water we were ex- pected to get, and I firmly believe we should not have had any extras if we had not had this unexpected quan- tity of water to deal with. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Law- son's contract at Winwick for wells and adits was £24,400, and it had to be regulated by a schedule of prices. Mr, Dees has requested use not to give any more figures, as we may have some litigation over this contract,and we ought not to sacrifice the interest of the ratepayers of Warrington in order to satisfy the idle curiosity of a few people whose sole object in life is to be for ever quarrelling_ and minding everybodys' business but their own. (Hear, bear, and laughter.) Alderman Evalcs seconded.

Councillor PEMBERTON said that at the last meeting of the Council he was told and told very strongly by the Chairman of the Water Committee that he was telling untruths when he said he could not get access to thepapers. Alderman SMETHURST: Is that in the resolution? The MAYOR: Let him speak. Let us see if he can make one straightforward speech. Councillor PEMBERTON continuing said he was a mem- ber of the Water Committee and could therefore claim to know something about contracts and gain certain information which he thought the public of Warrington should knew. He called the previous Friday at the water engineer's office and asked for some information with regard to Lawson's contract—the amount of work done and the amount paid to Lawson. He called at the Water Engineer's office again that morning when he was told by Mr. Dees that he could not see the contract and specifications. While he was talking the chairman of the Water Committee came in and said he could not see the papers giving as his reason that the contract was not finished and as there might be some disputes as to the contract it would not be wise to allow access to the papers at present. As a member of the Water Committee and of the Corpora- tion he did not think it a proper thing that he or any other member of the Corporation should not have access to any paper, document or book which existed. He was told he had made assertions which he could not prove and had therefore no right to make them. In that case he wanted to know what every member of the Council was entitled to know—how mail extra money had been spent. There was an item of £2,900 which was put down as overspent, and which, as it was not in the original Parliamentary power was rather misleading. On Lawson's account alone there was already overspent some £B,OOO. It was to ascertain where that money had gone that he made appli- cation to see the papers, and he thought it a breach of the privilege of the members of the Council that he should be denied access to those papers. The overspending in committees was a very serious matter. The chairman of the Water Committee told them they found too muchilwater so the committee put down one engine instead of two. All he could say was that they must have found out very soon that they had too much water as the engine was ordered two years ago, and over two years ago they let the contract for the building of a larger engine house whereas if they had had two smaller engines the old engine house would have been sufficient. He asked the Council to reject the motion until they had had the proper facts placed before them and until they knew exactly what Was the position-of the Water Committee in the matter. Alderman Francomb hoped the Corporation would hive larger custom for their water. He (the speaker) also hoped the same as they were likely to have a deficiency of something like £2,800 That was a very serious thing and if it had to be met immediately out of the rates it would mean something like OH. in the E. The MA.YOR : The Borough Treasurer says it is not so._

Councillor PEMBERTON : The figures were given by Mr. Fairhurst to our committee. If we pay theprincipal and interest we will spend something like £2,800 more than our income. p Alderman FRANCOMB : You must have been dreaming. Councillor PEMBERTON: I have often wished a short- hand report was taken of committee meetings and of what is said at such meetings because it is very annoy- ing for certain things to be said in committee, and when I repeat them in the Council to be told I am wronF. The MAYOR : That is not on the minutes. It is not argument. lam trying to keep as quiet as possible. Councillor SMETHURST : lam sure you are. Councillor Pemberton continuing said that in the original bill there was a sum set down for mains re- quired and for the extension of mains. What had be- come of the sum and how was it that the Chairman of the Water committee now asked for another £5,000 for r mains?

Alderman Pftiticoms : It has been spent in the ordinary way of business. Councillor PEMBERTON continuing, maintained that the Council should be taken fully into the confidence of ths chairman of the, committee before they author- ised that the money should be spent. He thought if he was to be trusted by the ratepayers he ought to be trusted with information with regard to Lawson's con- tract and ought not to be refused information to which he was entitled.

Councillor Loan: I must strongly protest against the action of the Chairman of the Water Committee and the Water Engineer. I think it is preposterous. Therefore it was impossible for CouncillorPemberton to see those documents. He thought that should be a satisfactory reason why he should be refused permission to see them. Councillor PEMBERTON: The contract was not sealed. ' I myself saw it on a table in the Water Engineer's office ; this morning. to produce figures with regard to Lawson's contract. That certifi- cate showed the committee were overspent to the extent of £2,400. The reason of that overspending had been explained and was, he considered, very satisfactory. Councillor Tmxiox : I find I misunderstood the officials as to the meaning of the word sealed. I under- stood that meant placed in an envelope and sealed, whereas it meant placed under the Corporation seal. The TOWN CLERK : The specifications and prices were actually sealed in an envelope. Councillor BARTON thought every member of the Council should have every facility to make himself acquainted with the business of the Council. and it should be understood that nothing should be withheld. The MAYOR: There are certain things which cannot be made public. Continuing he said the estimate was £147,000, whereas they would have to spend £167,510, because the estimated quantity of water was 1,250,000 gallons against 4,000,000 gallons which they actually got. It was estimated they would supply a population of 60,000 whereas they could supply a population of 200,000. It was the increase in the amount of water which necessitated , the extra expenditure. That money conld only be got by borrowing from the Local Government Board or by being directly obtained from the rates, and he did not think any member of the Council would say they should increase the rates by is. fid. in the £ and pay the money , at once.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS. Rugby Football. (By " Ovalist";

Warrington have spoiled their honourable record for the old year. Prior to Saturday they had not lost one of this season's home matches, but on absolutely the last day of 1091 they succumbed po Leigh and for- feited their badge of merit. I, and indeed all their supporters, had hoped that they would preserve the , spotless home certificate throughout the season, but the unexpected happened. The first meeting between Warrington and Leigh this season was the opening match of the tourney and Warrington triumphed by 16 points to 2 points. They consequently looked upon the return as a "good thing," but Fate upset the applecart so to speak. Not one of the regular three- quarters was available on Saturday, while several reserve men were also ineligible, so that the club was indeed in a sorry plight. They made a brave fight, however, and Leigh's win by 5 points to 2 was some- what lucky.

The match was of more than average importance for only one point separated the clubs in the South- West Lancashire Competition the trophy for which is in Warrington's possession. Consequently the struggle was a keen one from start to finish with the issue always in doubt. Warrington had a threequarter line cmsist- ing of Kenyon, Brooks, Hockenhall and Short, who gave a very fair account of themselves everything considered. But they lacked scoring power, as was manifest throughout the whole of the second half. The blame for the defeat lies primarily on the half backs, however. Di Davies and Duckworth are about the weakest pair Warrington could have selected, and they never made any headway throughout the game. They are poor tacklers in addition; as was plain when Burgess scored the winning try for Leigh. The efforts to stop him were, well, simply girlish.

It was a great blunder on the part of the Com- mittee to play these two. Either Forster or Thomas would have done far better, for both possess the dash and strength which was obviously -required and—they can tackle! I can't imagine Burgess forcing his way over the line with either of these two men in the vicinity. It seems to me that the changes have been rung often enough on nail backs whose failings are well known. Duckworth, for instance, though a grand trier, has neither the strength nor skill for the first team, and the fact has been well- known for years. Why the Committee should fall back on him is a puzzle, and it certainly reflects great discredit on their judgment. Let us see some pluck in the way of an experiment ! Put Hockenhall and Thomas behind the pack, and there will at any rate be some energy imported into this department of the game. The men whn have operated of late, with the single exception of Hockenhall, have been terribly feeble.

The•result of Saturday's match was a great disap- pointment to the Warrington crowd. They had looked forward to the humbling of the cocky Leighites but the process did not materialise. A different result c3old hardly be expected from the team Warrington put in the field, but the fact remains that the locals had far more of the game than the visitors after the first quarter of an hour. It was in this period that the damage was done. Leigh went off with desperate energy, and a series of attacks culminated in Burgess scoring against his old comrades. He had been simply dithering to get over, and weak tackling gave him his chance. Dunbavin placed an easy goal. Warring- ton's response was full of determination and Kenyon once got through but he had been in touch. Event- ually Pieston landed a penalty goal at the third attempt,and on turning round there were only 3 points between the teams.

Leigh were only in the home quarter twice in the second half so, strongly did the Warrington forwards play, and so well did Hallam kick. But they were content to defend, and the constant punt- ing into touch grew monotonous in the extreme. Once Warrington were very unlucky. A rush by the forwards caused Durthavin to miss his mark, and the ball was legitimately carried over, but a premature I whistle by the referee who thought the mark would be made, nullified the point. There were various efforts afterwards, but the halves made such poor use of the ball that Warrington were easily kept out.

The best men on the home side were undoubtedly Hallam and Hockenhall, who both did all that could be expected of them. Kenyon and Brookes did fairly well, in fact I might say that the wing man played excellently under the circumstances. He certainly I showed better form than heretofore. Among the ' forwards, who all worked hard, Thomas and Boardman were always prominent. The heeling out was slow at times, and here Leigh had an advantage which was of great service to Burgess and Dunbayin. These two played soundly, but they might have done more to make the game attractive. Johnson was the best of the Leigh threequarters and he kept the home defence on the qui vivi. As a team, however, Leigh did not create a very bright impression. They have not yet forsaken of the close tactics which detract so much from their play and keep scoring on either side down.

Leigh's success gives them the premier position in the South West Lancashire Competition by a point Had Warrington won they would hi ye held such a commanding position that they would prol9ably have had no difficulty in retaining the cup, but they will require now to fight hard for the laurels. Both teams have four matches to play so that anything may happen. Leigh's four matches, St. Helens, Wigan, Runcorn, and Widnes, are all away, while Warrington still have St. Helens at home, and Wigan, Runcorn, and Widnes away. The team with the best chance of finishing at the top, how- ever, is Wigan, for with five matches to play they have four at home, viz., St. Helens, Widnes, Warring-

ton, and Leigh, the away match being at Runcorn. The Competition is therefore in a very interesting state.

The South-West Lancashire League table up-to-date is as follows :

Oldham's position at the bead of the League table was somewhat weakened during the week-end by a defeat at Halifax, but they still possess a useful lead. Broughton Rangers missed a fine chance of shortly getting to the head of affairs when they failed to win at Wakefield on Saturday. The Trinitarians have thus taken four points from the Rangers—points which may make all the difference to the Wheater's field crowd when final positions come to be allocated.

Warrington have another visit to Yorkshire to-day, and in:the light of recent"performances their supporters will not have much hope of their Emcees. at Halifax. Warrington, nevertheless, should stand a fair chance if at all well represented on the field. They always play a good game at Hanson-lane, and in addition they will find the home team in probably a worse plight than themselves. Little, the full-back, played during a big part of the game with Oldham on Monday, with a fractured shoulder bone, which will keep him off tho football field for four or five weeks. This is had luck for Halifax, for they have already four players hors de combat, Drummond, with a fractured rib, Joe Riley with a damaged thigh, Nettleton suffering from ',Mod poisoning, and Wedgwood from a severe attack of influenza. As Leigh profited by Warrington's misfortunes last week, so may Warrington profit at Halifax. They should certainly go away with considerable hope. The absence of Little alone means a good deal to the I visiting side. In times past he has usually kicked a deciding goal against Warrington.

There have been some important alterations in the composition of the team for to-day's visit to Halifax Both halves have been dropped, and an experiment is made by selecting Short to partner Hockenhall behind the scrummage. I have not much hope of this change proving very successful, for Short lacks the required dash for such a position, but he should do as well as either of the men he has replaced. Isherwood and Fish return to the threeqnarter line, while Brooks and Kenyon retain their places. In the forwards Jolley takes the place of Cook.

The team is: Hallam, full back; Fish, Isherwood, Brooks and Kenyon, threeq carters; Flockenhall and Short, half backs; Boardman, Preston, Thotuas, Shugars, Belton, Jolley, Swift, and Harmer, forwards.

The "A" teauaers were very unfortunate last week Owing to the demands of the first fifteen they had to Iniake the journey to Leigh with only eleven men and were beaten by a goal to nil. Though so severely handicapped the Warrington lads had the best of the game and deserved to win. Forster played a poor game at half and lost several chances. He also spoiled his prospect of promotion to the first team. This defeat, added to the loss of a paint through the Chadderton match, may rob Warrington of the championship, for Wigan now hold a strung lead.

Hockey. [By R.G.P.]

Last week Stockton Heath journeyed to Sale with rather a weak team, being minus E. E. Woods, S. Baxter and Edo Fairclough, and in consequence had to submit to a severe beating, the horns side winning by no less than sto nil. For this defeat no excuse can be raised, except that Stockton Heath met a superior side.

The Sale ground was in beautiful condition, being almost fit to play tennis on, so even aud smooth was the turf. Now although this is an ideal state of things for good hockey, yet the Stockton Heath club are not accustomed to play on such a fast ground, where the ball comes running aloug just like a billiard ball, and can be at once played with the stick, without first lain the hands or feet,and were thus at a great disadva--

Ivantage.

For Stockton Heath the backs played a sound game, especially J. Richardson at centre-half. The!forwards played well for the first quarter of an hour, but after that went all to pieces, and could make no headway against the Sale halves. L. Robson playing a beautiful game, his stick work being very fine.

-- The second eleven, or rather a team turned out to represent the second, as what with the new year holi- days, players being taken as substitutes for the first, and three suffering from injuries, the side that turned out was weak, and consequently a strong second team like Sale had a very easy time and eventually ran out winners by 7-0.

-- However it is pleasing to hear that R. Dolan who turned out for first time at hockey, playeda good game Hawkins also played a good defensive game for the home side.

To-day the club play Timperley Ist and 2nd, when the following will represent Stockton Heath :-Ist (away), R. G. Pierpoint (goal), E. E. Woods and E. Pierpoint (backs), J. Richardson, H. Kirkconnel, and L. Fairclough (halves), C. H. Bond, S. Baxter, W. Longdin, W. Richardson and D. Skelton (forwards). Train leaves Arpley 1.13 (to Broadheath). Referee, Mr. N. W. Bellamy.

2nd (home), F. G. Kartland (goal), P. B. Hawtin and J. Oliffe (backs), A. Hodkinson (captain), H. Smith and G. Hatton (halves), A. N. Other, W. Tinnell, T. Cro ton, W. Howard, and A. Ferguson (forwards). Bully-oft 2.30.

Diagram 46 shows a run-through cannon from baulk. Though this cannon will look a very awkward one to the majority of players if the balls be set up in the positions given under the diagram, it is not really a very difficult stroke though one that always requires careful handling. In playing this stroke the cue ball should be struck low down and with medium pace and the player should centre his mind on playing the first ball just past the second and avoiding the kiss. If the kiss is only just avoided the cannon is almost certain to be made, but if the first ball misses the second ball by a con- siderable margin no cannon will be made. Diagrani 47 shows a difficult run-thrpugh in-off into a top pocket, a stroke hardly within the compass of ,any but good players. The distance between the bAlls and the angle at which the cue ball has to enter the pocket, make this run. throng. .in-.f( an awkward one even tor goad players. If. hrwtsvP-, he stroke be played with drag and side, it will be found easier of accom- plishment than if played with side only or side and tap. Played with drag the true running of the cu.s b al will be more assured and apart from this the cu o ball will retain the side longer than if played .without drag. (To be Continued.)
